Anthropic expands AI security plans for critical infrastructure
2026-10-09

Anthropic expands AI security plans for critical infrastructure

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Opt-in open-source projects will receive free periodic security scans with suggested fixes, the company said. Anthropic is expanding its cyber defence programme with new tools to scan open-source projects and a collaborative mission that brings its security expertise to big businesses. The AI giant and its contemporaries have been creating advanced models with advanced capabilities to identify and exploit vulnerabilities. These models have already hacked several government websites and businesses, while the technology could also be misused by bad actors to significantly boost their ability to carry out large-scale hacks or phishing attacks. Earlier this year, Anthropic launched Project Glasswing, a first-of-its-kind joint mission, alongside its cyber-focused model Mythos 5, to collaborate with major companies and government organisations to protect themselves against these newer AI-enabled risks (the EU only received access to Mythos last month after weeks of discussions with the company). According to Anthropic, under this project, the company and its industrial collaborators scanned hundreds of widely used open-source projects and privately reported their findings to the organisations involved. Now, it is expanding its efforts to launch an open-source software (OSS) scanner, an opt-in service similar to Google’s OSS-Fuzz. Projects that are enrolled under this will receive free periodic AI-generated security scans from Anthropic’s “most capable models”, the company said. The reports will include a proof of concept of how the bug could be exploited, an explanation and a suggested fix where one is available. The company is also launching a critical infrastructure defence programme which brings frontier Claude models and on-site engineers to select providers, including Accenture, CrowdStrike, Deloitte, PwC and Palo Alto Networks among the project’s other founding partners. Several partners are currently working with Claude to fix vulnerabilities and help customers do the same, the company said. Last month, Anthropic tapped Accenture to independently evaluate its frontier AI developments after CEO Dario Amodei called for better transparency across the sector. The partnership will be led by Accenture’s specialist AI business Faculty, which will conduct independent assessments and model safeguards testing at Anthropic. Silicon Republic
Rare nitrogen molecule could be key to better water quality testing
2026-10-09

Rare nitrogen molecule could be key to better water quality testing

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Dr Alan Seltzer testing water samples. Image: University College Dublin New study ‘opens the door to a much more complete picture of the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems’, says UCD’s Dr Alan Seltzer. Scientists have found a new way to improve water-quality assessments using a rare form of nitrogen gas. Nitrogen is key to life on Earth, but it is only helpful in the right amounts. For example, a high concentration of nitrogen in water can lead to poor water quality and the gradual death of aquatic animals. Tiny microbes living in water can remove nitrogen by converting it into a gas that escapes into the atmosphere – though the amount of nitrogen this process removes is something researchers have often struggled to determine. In a new study co-authored by University College Dublin’s Dr Alan Seltzer, researchers have shown that a rare form of nitrogen gas can act as a natural fingerprint for microbial nitrogen conversion. Seltzer is an assistant professor of hydrogeology and groundwater systems at the university. “The problem is that our water naturally has a huge amount of nitrogen gas that is dissolved from the air, so the gas that microbes produce can be very difficult to see,” explained first author Jiarui Liu, who conducted the research as a postdoctoral fellow at University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) and University of California, Santa Barbara. “The answer is written in the way nitrogen atoms are paired inside the nitrogen molecule.” The paper was published in the journal Science yesterday (8 October). According to the study, the rare, naturally found 15N15N isotopologue can be used to determine the amount of nitrogen production in water, offering scientists a new tool to improve water-quality assessments and estimate global nitrogen budgets. Nitrogen gas – or N2 – consists of two nitrogen atoms joined together, UCD explained. These come in two weights, also called isotopes; the common nitrogen-14 and the heavier nitrogen-15. Most N2 molecules contain two nitrogen-14 atoms, while some contain one of each isotope. In very rare circumstances, both atoms are nitrogen-15. In atmospheric nitrogen gas, the two heavy nitrogen-15 atoms pair up more often than expected, while the nitrogen gas produced by microbes has atoms paired nearly at random. Scientists have figured out that when nitrogen from microbes mixes with nitrogen from the air, it reduces that excess of heavy pairs, creating a relative abundance of the rare molecule that can reveal how much nitrogen gas was produced by microbial activity. The team measured nitrogen gas extracted from water and sediment samples using UCLA’s Panorama mass spectrometer. The instrument uses electric and magnetic fields to separate molecules according to their mass and charge. “At UCLA, we discovered the anomalous pairing of heavy nitrogen atoms in Earth’s atmosphere and are now making use of this signature of nitrogen in air as a powerful and unique geochemical tool,” said geochemist Edward Young, one of the paper’s co-authors and Liu’s postdoctoral adviser at UCLA. “We want to understand whether microbes in groundwater can mitigate nitrate pollution, and how much nitrogen is removed along the way before it can fuel algal growth in lakes, rivers and coastal waters,” Liu said. “This gives us a clearer picture of whether nitrogen stays in an ecosystem or is removed from the nutrient pool as N2 gas.” Seltzer added: “Many routine groundwater-quality monitoring programmes do not measure the N2 gas produced within an aquifer, which can create substantial biases in our accounting of where nitrogen comes from and where it goes. “This study, and this exciting new technique, opens the door to a much more complete picture of the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems.” Seltzer was recently awarded a European Research Council Starting Grant for his project called TabPhish that traces groundwater focusing on water level changes and nitrogen cycling. This research will expand on his collaboration with UCLA. “This first application provides a strong foundation for our upcoming work and represents just the start of a powerful new toolkit for better understanding the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems impacted by human activity,” he said. Silicon Republic
How can the CyberSafe Kids report help create a secure ecosystem?
2026-10-09

How can the CyberSafe Kids report help create a secure ecosystem?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Deloitte’s Nicola Flannery discusses how we can contribute to the creation of a safer online world for young people and the wider community. CyberSafe Kids, in partnership with Deloitte, recently published the 10th annual edition of a report exploring the digital lives of children in Ireland, providing an overview of how they spend their time online, the level of access they have and their overall experiences – both positive and negative. The latest report, which is titled ‘The Year Little Changed’, collected data from more than 7,000 children aged eight to 15 across 74 Irish schools during the 2025 to 2026 academic year. This was combined with additional focus group discussions and independent research conducted by students from Atlantic Technological University and University College Dublin. The aim of the research is to build a larger understanding of trends and risks impacting young people online. It also calls on its audience to create a safer online environment for young people. Commenting on the report and its broader potential, Nicola Flannery, a partner for digital trust and privacy, technology and transformation at Deloitte, explained that the report being in its 10th year is significant. She told SiliconRepublic.com, “This isn’t a one-off snapshot, it’s a decade of tracking how children in Ireland actually experience the online world, which means we can see the direction of travel rather than just a single moment in time. “This year’s edition, The Year Little Changed, is particularly significant because it measures what has actually improved since major regulatory changes came into effect.” Alex Cooney, CEO and co-founder of CyberSafe Kids, added, “This year’s report marks a decade of monitoring the online risks and vulnerabilities facing children and young people in Ireland. And the message from this year’s data is clear – despite a raft of new legislation in Ireland and at the EU level in 2025, very little has changed in relation to children’s online safety one year on.” Flannery agreed, noting that despite major regulatory changes such as the Online Safety Code and the Digital Services Act (DSA) coming into effect, children in Ireland are still encountering many of the same harms in the same spaces, “without meaningfully better protection than they had before any of this legislation existed”. “That’s the wake-up call at the heart of the report and it’s a difficult one for anyone who assumed the regulatory groundwork of the last couple of years would already be showing results. I’d say almost everyone with a stake in this space needs to sit with it,” she said. Flannery suggested that policymakers, regulators, parents, educators, platform leaders, technologists and business leaders across the wider STEM sectors have a responsibility to read reports such as this and educate themselves on the changes their specific position requires them to make. Broader society could also familiarise itself, she noted, “because a lot of this comes down to a business model built around engagement that hasn’t been sufficiently weighed against the cost to children and that’s a conversation that goes well beyond any one regulator or platform”. According to Flannery, a number of key findings emerged from the report, such as the issues around age assurance, which was implemented as a means of ensuring younger children are not exposed to age-inappropriate material. She is of the opinion that the measure simply isn’t working as it was meant to, as the report indicates 70pc of participating eight- to 12-year-olds continue to own accounts on platforms that require users to be at least 13. “Self-declaration remains the main method platforms rely on, which means a child only has to type in a different birth year to get past it – and clearly, a lot of them are doing exactly that without anyone noticing,” she said. “What’s interesting is that the [European] Commission seems to agree this is the core problem. Its proposed EU Kids Act states plainly that self-declared age isn’t enough, and would require certified, independent age verification instead, alongside a default minimum age of 15 for opening a social media account without a parent involved. “It’s still a proposal rather than law and unlikely to actually apply for another couple of years, but it’s a clear signal that Brussels has looked at exactly the gap this report is describing and decided the current approach has failed.” Popular gaming platform Roblox came up again and again in the research, with Flannery noting this is for good reason, as it is the platform where young children in Ireland spend the greatest amount of time. Almost 60pc of negative interactions reported by children aged eight to 12 were found to have happened on Roblox and included inappropriate avatars, sexual content, grooming attempts, scams, and games built around self-harm themes with no safeguards in place. Flannery said, “For a long time, Roblox sat outside the tightest layer of DSA scrutiny, though that’s actually just changed. The European Commission designated it a Very Large Online Platform at the end of August, which brings a stricter set of obligations into play from early next year. “That’s a step in the right direction, but designation on paper and genuinely safer experiences for an eight-year-old are still two different things, and that gap is exactly what this report is measuring.” Unsurprisingly, AI also presents a challenge to protecting young people online. More than half of the eight-to-12 cohort and 76pc of 12- to 15-year-olds said that they use AI tools, and nearly half admitted that no one has ever had a conversation with them about artificial intelligence at all. Flannery said, “We spent the last decade rolling out social media to children faster than we built the safeguards around it and we appear to be making precisely the same choice with AI, at a faster pace and with less public conversation about what’s actually at stake. “It’s worth saying that the proposed EU Kids Act doesn’t stop at social media either. It explicitly covers AI companions and chatbots, with specific restrictions around emotional dependency and memory features aimed at younger users. “That’s a genuinely forward-looking piece of drafting, but again, it’s not law yet, and children are forming these habits with AI right now, well ahead of any protection actually landing.” Viewed collectively, she explained that harm is never confined to just one form of content or platform. Rather, it is structural, and permeates the entire digital ecosystem to which children already have significant access. “Regulation as it currently stands is proving to be necessary but nowhere near sufficient on its own.” Cooney added, “We still see high levels of underage access to social media platforms with a 13-plus age rating, and the same numbers reporting upsetting experiences (25pc) and bullying behaviour (28pc). The regulation designed to protect children online either isn’t working, isn’t fit for purpose or isn’t being sufficiently enforced – or a combination of all three.” Making the environment safer for vulnerable young people requires accepting a level of responsibility that moves far beyond issues of compliance. Flannery finds that the STEM leaders shaping the digital infrastructure that people have come to depend on need to be conscious of how their choices in design, algorithm development and business models have major downstream impacts on society, posing real harm to children. “The trouble is that the current model treats child safety as a constraint to be managed rather than a design principle to be embedded from the start. Platforms build in safety measures after something has already gone wrong, responding to harm instead of preventing it, and that’s the wrong way round,” she said. “STEM leaders have both the power and, I’d argue, the obligation to do things differently. That starts with embedding safety by design as a foundational principle rather than a bolt-on feature, which means age-appropriate design as standard, no engagement-maximising algorithms aimed at children, no behavioural profiling, no dark patterns.” She thinks it also means pushing back on regulatory timelines that are blatantly unrealistic and instead focusing on what is genuinely achievable. This means investing properly in research and talent, and being upfront about necessary trade-offs, in her view. Flannery noted, “Safety often sits in tension with engagement and monetisation, and leaders need to be transparent about where they stand rather than letting that tension go unspoken. “Parents, educators, policymakers and children themselves are looking to STEM leaders to do the right thing here – and right now, the evidence suggests that isn’t happening on the scale it needs to.” Cooney concluded, “More than 7,300 children were surveyed, providing crucial first-hand insights into the realities of growing up online today. What some of these children describe encountering online is deeply concerning and it highlights the urgency of the work still needed to make their online world safer.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. You give it objectives, not instructions. “You delegate an outcome and come back to finished work. For an agent to do that, it has to be connected to your personal workflows, your systems of record and your enterprise controls. “Gemini can function as your personal assistant or as a team member, where it works on behalf of a group of people, like a project manager within a team, or on behalf of a specific role in an organisation, like an analyst in your finance department.” According to Google, the agent can connect to a company’s enterprise systems, organise workload, access tools and return completed work to the necessary location. Crunchbase: European start-up VC sees best quarter in four years – again
2026-10-09

Crunchbase: European start-up VC sees best quarter in four years – again

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
AI drove 75pc of European funding this past quarter, according to date from Crunchbase. Europe’s start-up scene recently enjoyed its best quarter for venture funding in four years, according to business data tracking platform Crunchbase. Q3 2026 saw total start-up funding in the continent reach $25bn, up around 77pc in year-on-year terms from Q3 2025, during which $14bn was invested in the sector. The latest total is marginally higher than Crunchbase reported for Q2 of this year, which also marked the best quarter in a four-year period at the time, at $24bn in venture funding. AI drove 75pc of European funding this past quarter, according to Crunchbase, with an investment total of $18.8bn marking the highest proportion on record. This is up from around $12bn in the previous quarter, and significantly higher than the $7bn in AI funding from Q3 2025. The UK led Europe’s quarterly funding totals at $7.5bn. Germany ($5bn) and France ($4.8bn) gained market share for venture capital funding during Q3, with each posting their strongest quarters since early 2022. Sweden ($1.5bn), the Netherlands ($1.4bn) and Spain ($1.2bn) enjoyed the next highest start-up investment totals. “Europe represented 16pc of global venture capital in Q3, although the true impact of its technology and start-ups is higher, with many of the region’s start-ups expanding or moving to the US to capitalise on the rapid growth of AI there,” Crunchbase’s report noted. Its data is based on reported information as of 5 October. Four individual European companies raised billion-dollar-plus funding during the most recent quarter, representing close to 40pc of the region’s total start-up capital for the period. France’s AI champion Mistral said its $3.5bn September Series D raise was “the largest equity fundraising round ever completed by a European technology company”. Earlier this week, it announced a new open-weight AI model that it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. UK AI infrastructure developer Nscale raised a convertible note of $3.36bn that it plans to convert upon going public, according to Crunchbase. German defence-tech start-ups Helsing and Quantum Systems raised $1.8bn and $1.2bn respectively in the past quarter. A total of $17.3bn, or 70pc of Europe’s Q3 venture capital, was invested in late-stage deals across 83 companies, according to Crunchbase, which defines this type of funding as Series C, D, E and beyond, venture rounds of unknown series, corporate venture, and other rounds more than $15m. “Along with growth in AI funding, late-stage funding noticeably increased last quarter, with many of the leading rounds sourced across multiple European countries,” the report said. Early-stage funding – defined as Series A and B rounds, rounds of unknown series, corporate venture, and other rounds more than $3m and less than or equal to $15m – came to $5.7bn. This total for Q3, spread across 200 or so companies, was down from Q2 and similar to the same period in 2025, while being shared among fewer individual deals than previous quarters. Europe’s seed funding, meanwhile, fell quarter-over-quarter and was flat year-over-year, with around $2bn invested in roughly 750 companies at seed stage during Q3. Mainstream News Anchors Now Talking About the AI Bubble Bursting at Any Moment
2026-10-10

Mainstream News Anchors Now Talking About the AI Bubble Bursting at Any Moment

Futurism | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
On Thursday, news that ChatGPT maker OpenAI was set to miss its annual revenue goal by a cool $20 billion sent stock markets into a tailspin. The tech-heavy Nasdaq index took the heaviest hit, falling 1.25 percent by the close of trading. Of course, the stock market happily bounced back to a near-record high as soon as the markets opened on Friday, but the whole episode underscores how fragile the AI spending boom really is — and how little information we truly have from private AI labs which have no obligation to keep the public up to speed. Perhaps jolted by the momentary slump, producers for Bloomberg TV‘s daily Open Interest broadcast brought on Joachim Klement, managing director and research analyst at investment bank Panmure Liberum. As a financial analyst, Klement’s take on the AI economy has been notably grim — back in May, he penned an article in Reuters arguing that institutional investors shouldn’t be asking “whether the AI boom will end, but what will happen to markets when it does.” Now, his live interview on Bloomberg‘s flagship news channel underscores the degree to which the AI bubble is becoming impossible for mainstream commentators to ignore, even as some publications cite tech billionaires who insist the AI bubble is a myth. “My core conviction is that the AI bubble will either burst in 2027 or in 2028,” Klement was quoted saying in his Bloomberg appearance. That conviction is prefaced on the belief that the US AI bubble is focused on the wrong kind of technology: namely, resource-intensive frontier models. “I think the entire AI boom is investing in the wrong future of AI,” Klement said. “At the moment we’re investing into frontier large language models running in data centers, when it becomes, to me, more and more clear that the real future of AI is small language and open-weight models running on local desktop computers. And that, to me, indicates that we’re already over-invested in data centers as more and more of these applications move to smaller, cheaper models running locally.” “I think we’ve over-invested and the realization will come that these earnings,” Klement continued, “that some people — who shall remain nameless at this point in time — expect to fill the prices, and earnings to grow into the prices, they won’t materialize.” When Bloomberg anchor Dani Burger raised the elephant in the room — the trillions of dollars worth of data center infrastructure planned for the next few years — Klement explained that the entire investment ultimately depends on just a few tech companies whose grand plans may never pan out. “When it comes to the underlying demand, if you strip out OpenAI and Anthropic’s demand, there’s hardly any demand there for AI compute that justifies the trillion-dollar investments that the hyperscalers alone are expected to do next year,” Klement replied. To call Klement’s forecast “gloomy” would be an understatement. With the fate of the US economy now completely fused with the data center boom, any slowdown in AI spending will almost certainly spell disaster for everybody — not just the tech industry. “If you had asked me two months ago, I would have said ‘that’s a bubble that can easily last another two years,'” Klement said. Now, “all I need for this bubble to burst is for growth plans to be revised downward, and for growth to slow down, because that already will change the earning’s outlook for basically the entire supply chain.” More on AI: AI Is Creating “Doom Loops” All Over the Economy

Walmart Running Into Trouble as Its Super-Expensive Robots Can’t Handle the Realities of Warehouse Work
2026-10-10

Walmart Running Into Trouble as Its Super-Expensive Robots Can’t Handle the Realities of Warehouse Work

Futurism | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Despite spending billions of dollars to automate its warehouses, Walmart is seriously struggling to make even the simplest of robots work. As the Wall Street Journal reports, even something as straightforward as removing an object from a cardboard box to package it for customers is proving exceedingly challenging. After hiring several robotics companies, Walmart ultimately settled on small robots with wheels for over a dozen of its warehouses. But the small robots are still unable to remove larger and heavier objects, like bags of dog food. They also break down often, showing that despite plenty of pressure to replace human workers, companies are running into major headwinds in automating real-world workflows in environments like warehouses and logistics facilities. Executives maintain that Walmart’s automation push will result in fewer warehouse workers, transitioning many of them into managing and fixing robots. However, that transition is reportedly coming at a formidable cost. According to the WSJ‘s sources at Walmart, the costs of paying mechanical and electrical engineers at its automated warehouses eclipse the payrolls of unskilled warehouse workers. It’s not just Walmart. Amazon is also going to incredible lengths to automate its warehouses, creating what market-intelligence firm Interact Analysis’ Rueben Scriven told the WSJ amounts to an “arms race” between retailers. Yet despite major financial commitments, many of these efforts are crumbling. Case in point, Kroger had to write off a whopping $2.6 billion after a major investment in an “automated fulfillment network” failed to pay off. Meanwhile, a far more ambitious transition to humanoid warehouse robots feels like even more of a stretch. While companies are pumping billions of dollars into the development of bipedal bots, the tech largely remains a Moonshot for practical physical labor. Mark Zuckerberg Launched Meta’s Muse AI Agent Despite Disturbing Safety Concerns

Visit Original Website
When Meta first launched its cutesy Muse AI agents to the public, the company was immediately met with a virtual tidal wave of headlines, as major errors and glaring privacy issues rose to the surface. As it turns out, the decision to release Muse was far from unanimous. As fresh reporting from the New York Times reveals, the buggy agent was forced onto the market by its CEO Mark Zuckerberg, even as it went rogue on the very people who built it. According to the NYT, Muse’s development cycle was plagued with disturbing safety errors that delayed its release for months. It was only after the release of Instinct, a rival AI agent developed by a tiny startup, that Zuckerberg decided to pull the trigger in a pique of corporate FOMO. As three anonymous insiders told the NYT, there was one case during testing in which Muse changed a test user’s password without permission. In another example, a Meta researcher named Summer Yue realized too late that her test agent had taken command of her work computer and was deleting her emails en masse. “I couldn’t stop it from my phone,” Yue said in a post on X-formerly-Twitter. “I had to RUN to my Mac mini like I was defusing a bomb.” Those safety issues were still top-of-mind in the days leading up to Muse’s debut, as engineers discovered a glaring security flaw allowing the AI bot to “break in” to Meta’s own internal database, which staff feared could be exploited by hackers. That’s significant — not just for Meta’s own security, but for the safety of users. As it turns out, Muse is designed to suck up reams of data on its users, as well as friends and family in a user’s orbit. Time will tell what other safety issues bubble to the surface as more and more users hand over their lives to Meta’s buzzy AI toy. But with oversight like this, it’s almost certainly a matter of when, not if. Police Furious After an Anthropic AI Model Submitted a Bogus Tip About an Unsolved Murder, Posing as a Person Who Might Have Information About the Case

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
An Anthropic AI model submitted a bogus tip about an unsolved murder to the Philadelphia police, in a blunder the department called “unacceptable.” The unspecified model, not unlike an unscrupulous prankster, submitted the fake info about the cold case through PhillyUnsolvedMurders.com on July 18. Anthropic, however, didn’t find out about it until September 28 — and further waited until October 8 to inform police about what happened. The police didn’t act on the information because it was automatically marked as spam. But the sham AI tip alarmed police officials all the same. “The company must strengthen its safeguards to prevent similar incidents from impacting city systems without the city’s knowledge,” the department said in a statement, per ABC6 News. “The two-month delay in detecting and reporting the incident to the City is unacceptable.” Anthropic hasn’t publicly responded to the news. But the company reportedly told police that it plans to publish a report about the incident, and other instances of its unintended behavior, later today. According to Anthropic, via the police statement, its model was conducting a test involving interactions with randomly selected websites when it chanced upon PhillyUnsolvedMurders.com. In the bogus tip it submitted, the AI posed as someone who might have information about the case. Though the department stressed that tips are treated as potential leads, not facts, and that it has procedures in place to vet them, it was alarmed at the advent of automated tools clogging up its backlog. “Those PPD safeguards limited the impact of this incident. They do not diminish the seriousness of an AI system presenting fabricated information as though it came from a person with knowledge of a homicide,” it said. “Unsolved cases involve real victims, grieving families and investigators working to secure answers.” It’s the latest example of the collateral damage from how frontier AI labs have tested their models, which have broken containment and hacked other companies and even government websites, with public typically not informed until months after the fact. Tesla Basically Just Admitted It Was Misleading Customers, Renames Full Self-Driving to “Tesla Assisted Driving” In Europe
2026-10-09

Tesla Basically Just Admitted It Was Misleading Customers, Renames Full Self-Driving to “Tesla Assisted Driving” In Europe

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Tesla has admitted a major defeat in its branding. No longer will its notorious driving software be called “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)” in Europe. After negotiations with Germany’s transportation ministry, it’s agreed to rename it “Tesla Assisted Driving,” in a more accurate representation of the software’s capabilities — and a symbolically humiliating submission by Elon Musk’s automaker. The concession, however, will pave the way for Tesla to finally release FSD in European markets, so it’s a tradeoff that Musk will happily accept after years of campaigning to make it happen. The rename has quickly taken effect. Across its European websites in countries including Germany, Spain, and even outside the European Union in the UK, all mentions of FSD now refer to “Tesla Assisted Driving,” Reuters reports. European users report that the new name is being shown in Tesla apps. The rebranding came after German transport minister Steffen Bilger announced Tuesday that he would back approval of FSD across Europe, after a lengthy discussion process with the automaker. “My goal is ​for Tesla drivers to be ⁠able to enjoy the benefits of ‘assisted driving’ throughout the EU without delay,” said Bilger. The change of heart didn’t come out of nowhere. A previous Reuters investigation detailed a heavy-handed social media campaign orchestrated by Tesla to smear European regulators as blocking life-saving technology, while also pressuring them to lighten their safety reviews and accept Tesla’s own research on the safety of its systems. Musk himself, meanwhile, has repeatedly made catastrophizing statements on X to his hundreds of millions of followers, claiming that delaying the approval of FSD “will cost lives.” That’s despite the numerous lawsuits and federal investigations his automaker is facing over the safety of FSD, including over fatal incidents. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration launched an investigation into nearly three million Teslas equipped with FSD after receiving reports of the cars violating traffic laws, including running red lights, driving the wrong way, and nearly driving into trains at railway crossings. Beyond safety, one of the things that has rubbed European regulators the wrong way is how the system’s name suggests that it can fully drive itself, when in reality it requires constant supervision in case the driver needs to take over at a moment’s notice.  Bilger said Tesla offered to rename the system to “Tesla Assisted Driving” to address the criticism that the name could be misleading. FSD’s spurious branding has caused friction with US regulators, too. The California DMV sued Tesla for alleged fake advertising because of FSD’s name. Amid the legal battle and regulatory scrutiny, Tesla quietly renamed Full Self-Driving to “Full Self-Driving (Supervised).” In China, it similarly redubbed the system “Intelligent Assisted Driving.” It’s a striking, though not unprecedented, reversal from Tesla to rebrand FSD. Portraying the cars as fully autonomous is important to Musk, as he frequently asserts that they can “drive themselves.” Tesla even sued the California DMV for “wrongfully and baselessly” calling it a “false advertiser” for using FSD. It evidently doesn’t believe enough in its own tech’s capabilities, though, to stick to its guns in Europe. More on Tesla: Elon Musk Admits Why Tesla Robotaxis Can’t Be Trusted at Night

OpenAI Is Wargaming Its Response in Case of Public Revolt After Catastrophic AI Disaster

OpenAI Is Wargaming Its Response in Case of Public Revolt After Catastrophic AI Disaster

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
In the United States, public sentiment against the rising power of AI labs and their CEOs is overwhelmingly negative. As Wall Street Journal polling from late September found, 63 percent of US adults support an immediate pause on AI development, while a Politico survey found about two-thirds of Americans have at least “moderate” anxieties over AI destroying humanity. When the scope of that public backlash first came into focus, tech billionaires like Mark Zuckerberg responded with some tone-deaf media stunts, hoping to win back hearts and minds by the millions. Now that this strategy has clearly failed, executives at top AI labs are starting to workshop standard operating procedures in the event of a massive revolt against them. This includes leaders at Anthropic and OpenAI, according to Axios, which broke the story. These amount to Pentagon-style “war games,” essentially, with AI labs hammering out responses to hypothetical scenarios like massive cyberattacks enabled by their products, or a political revolt in reaction to some sort of catastrophic event triggered by their models. Approached by Axios for comment, OpenAI was nonchalant about the whole affair, replying that “OpenAI conducts preparedness exercises where teams discuss and work through a range of potential scenarios.” “These scenarios are not treated as inevitable, but are meant to help us prepare for a variety of circumstances,” the spokesperson continued. Anthropic declined to comment, though anxiety about social unrest isn’t out of character for the company’s executives. In September, it was reported that Anthropic was building out a surveillance apparatus to keep tabs on anti-AI protest movements, which included taking a “pre-crime” approach to intelligence gathering. How serious a threat any of this actually is remains hard to say. Nonetheless, the fact that top tech execs are even considering the possibility is notable — a sign that if nothing else, the anti-AI backlash has them squirming in their board room chairs. Here are the top AI agents that can live in your text messages
2026-10-10

Here are the top AI agents that can live in your text messages

TechCrunch | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Rather than downloading another app, a growing number of agents can simply be texted like an ordinary person. You text it what you need, and it can remember context, connect to the apps and services you already use, and complete tasks on your behalf. That can mean scheduling an appointment, organizing a calendar, researching a trip, sending an email, making a reservation, shopping online, or reminding you about something days later. While Instinct is one of the buzziest AI agents at present, thanks to its $10 billion valuation after its latest funding round of $1 billion, there are many others also making a play for this space. Below are the most notable options so far, from general-purpose personal assistants to agents designed for families, travel, and work. Caddy is an AI assistant that turns the information scattered across your phone into things you can actually act on. It lives in iMessage for iPhone users and RCS for Android users, so there’s no separate app or inbox to constantly check. For instance, an email contains an appointment that needs to be added to your calendar or a friend sends a list of things to pick up. Instead of worrying about all the details getting buried, Caddy connects to your calendars and conversations, then identifies things that may require action. It can add events to your calendar, set reminders, keep track of follow-ups, and even do research for you. Caddy has been available in public beta since April 2026. Comma is designed to manage tasks across both work and everyday life. Comma carries tasks through to completion, checking its own work and determining when a task is finished. Completed work is surfaced for review when a decision or approval is needed. It also proactively notifies users and recommends the next steps. It can work across a user’s browser, computer, and files, while remaining accessible remotely through messaging apps such as Signal, Telegram, and WeChat. Comma maintains context and memory across devices and conversations, and can deliver daily briefings, flag urgent matters, and summarize recorded calls. Fambot is an AI-powered “chief of staff” for families, designed to bring together the many moving parts of family life, including school communications, sports activities, meal planning, calendars, and other day-to-day responsibilities, and turn them into an organized plan. The service is designed to work across both an app and text messages. Fambot is available on iOS devices, Android devices, and the web, and families can also interact with it directly over SMS. Every night, Fambot automatically sends a summary of the following day, including upcoming events, to-dos, and details such as school uniform or packing requirements. Parents can reply to those messages to ask questions, check information, or make changes to their calendar. Fambot currently connects to Gmail, Google Calendar, Outlook, and WhatsApp, with support for Apple Calendar planned for the future. The company launched in beta in early September 2026 and has raised $3.5 million in pre-seed funding. The service is currently free during the beta period, but Fambot expects to eventually charge roughly the equivalent of a Netflix subscription. Folk is designed to be an AI assistant you can reach through iMessage, WhatsApp, and Telegram. It can remember personal context, manage reminders, research topics, track flights, handle email, and perform real-world tasks such as restaurant reservations. One distinction is that Folk runs on its own private cloud computer. It can also run code and execute multi-step tasks rather than merely responding conversationally. Folk launched its beta in May 2026, and it’s available for free but also offers a Pro subscription for $8.33/month if you want unlimited tasks running in the background. Instinct first made headlines after raising $350 million at a $2.5 billion valuation. In September 2026, it raised $1 billion, valuing it at $10 billion. The always-available agent can take actions on your behalf, rather than simply answer questions. Users can communicate with Instinct by text or voice and connect it to the apps and services they already use, such as email, calendar, Google Workspace, and more. The company says early users have used Instinct for tasks ranging from planning trips and buying groceries to booking tickets and canceling subscriptions. In September, Instinct began giving users dedicated email addresses for their assistants. The idea is to let Instinct create and manage accounts on a user’s behalf without filling the user’s personal inbox. The agent can use its address to sign up for services, contact businesses, follow up on requests, and handle tasks that require an email account. (However, this level of autonomy has also raised concerns about privacy and security.) It’s also begun rolling out support for placing calls. Powered by Hermes Agent, Iris is designed to let users delegate tasks to an AI assistant directly through iMessage. Users can connect the apps they use and hand over a task with a simple message. The agent then works through the required steps, while retaining relevant context from previous interactions. It’s also designed to follow up with users proactively rather than waiting for another request. Martin is a general-purpose personal assistant reachable through SMS as well as phone, WhatsApp, email, Slack, and its own iOS app. It can help manage calendars, email, reminders, tasks, notes, and communications. It can also act on behalf of users by sending messages or making phone calls, while providing daily summaries of what needs attention. It’s a bit on the pricier side, with subscriptions starting at $21 per month. Miso takes the personal-assistant concept and applies it specifically to travel. It operates through iMessage and combines AI trip planning with support from a dedicated travel team. Users can arrange flights through messaging while Miso takes into account their travel preferences, loyalty points, and other priorities. Miso also gives travelers access to trip details and flight updates, along with personalized recommendations. Ohai is another family assistant focused on helping households manage the constant stream of information and tasks involved in everyday family life. Users can send messages, forward emails, or make voice requests, and the assistant can transform them into schedules, reminders, and plans. It can also coordinate calendars, household chores, and meals across multiple members of a family. It includes a free basic option, but also offers different subscriptions based on family size, starting at $9.99 per month. Similar to the other family-focused agents, the idea behind Ollie is that families already have the information they need, but it lives across school messages, calendars, task lists, and group chats. Ollie aims to bring those pieces together and keep them in sync. It can keep track of calendars, emails, reminders, tasks, and other household details, then deliver relevant information to users through text. Its biggest differentiator from other agents is that Ollie is one of the first mainstream, family-focused AI assistants to achieve SOC 2 compliance, a widely used security standard. Launched in June 2026, Ollie offers a free tier, as well as paid plans starting at $25 per month for 150 messages. A $100-per-month plan includes up to 1,000 messages. Orbits also has an AI assistant that is focused on the household. It works through both the Orbits app and text messaging, and can bring together calendars, lists, and conversations while also carrying out tasks for you. That can include searching the web, making purchases, booking restaurants, changing appointments, and requesting quotes for household services. Additionally, Orbits can remind family members about chores, communicate with service providers, and help coordinate responsibilities beyond the immediate household. Orbits is backed by investors like Andreessen Horowitz (a16z) Speedrun fund, N49P, and Garage Capital. Users can interact with Pally as they would with a friend while giving it access to the apps they already rely on. The assistant can connect information spread across services such as WhatsApp, Gmail, calendars, and Google Drive, helping users keep track of plans, receipts, dates, files, and other details. There’s a free plan available, which offers features like 15 minutes of calls per month, or there are paid plans, including $25 per month for 30 minutes of calls and $100 per month for 60 minutes. Poke launched in March 2026 as a general-purpose assistant that you can reach through text messaging. It can handle tasks involving calendars, planning, health and fitness, smart-home controls, photos, and other everyday activities. Poke became particularly notable in June 2026 when Apple approved Poke as the first AI agent on the Apple Messages for Business platform. A month later, parent company The Interaction Company of California was acquired by AI coding startup Cognition in a deal valued in the low nine figures. Rene is an AI personal assistant designed to be a user’s “best contact” rather than another productivity agent. It’s text-based and can turn natural-language conversations into tasks, reminders, summaries, and other follow-up actions. Rene also maintains context across conversations, allowing it to remember details and surface things that may need attention later. Additionally, it has a browser, can write code, shop for you, as well as create slides and images. Rene is currently available on iMessage, Telegram, and WhatsApp. Instead of waiting for users to open an app, Skye is an “agentic homescreen” for the iPhone that analyzes information from connected services and surfaces what it thinks is relevant through contextual cards, suggestions, and daily briefings. The assistant can generate audio briefings, prepare information ahead of meetings, surface reminders, and identify potentially useful information. The company, Signull Labs, raised about $3.6 million in seed funding in 2025. It entered private testing and beta in April 2026, and hasn’t yet announced a public release date. Stanley is an AI content assistant aimed at content creators rather than general personal productivity. Available through iMessage, Telegram, and the web, it acts as a kind of AI head of content, helping users come up with ideas, turn them into posts, and maintain a consistent publishing schedule. For instance, Stanley can connect to a user’s Instagram account, using that context to suggest content based on what the user has actually been doing. Users can also send voice notes, which Stanley turns into scripts, captions, and other social assets. It includes a content calendar and can schedule posts, with the broader goal of making content creation a more continuous, automated process rather than something users have to sit down and plan from scratch. Szn is designed to coordinate tasks and follow-ups through a single conversation thread. Users can send text messages, voice notes, photos, or forwarded emails, and the assistant takes action on their behalf, including making calls, sending emails, and managing follow-ups. According to the company’s website, Szn has served more than 7,500 businesses across 16 countries, completed over 10,000 jobs, and handled 95 hours of calls. Tab allows users to delegate everyday tasks through familiar messaging platforms. Users can send requests to the assistant via iMessage or WhatsApp, ranging from ordering groceries to finding birthday gifts, and Tab handles the process on their behalf. The company emerged from stealth in early October with a valuation of $300 million. Its investors include SV Angel, Valar Ventures, and American Spirit. The biggest differentiator with Tomo’s personal assistant for iMessage is that, instead of being a private assistant that only you interact with, Tomo can become part of group conversations. Also, like other agents, it’s designed to help with goals, accountability, scheduling, and everyday organization. It also has the ability to send reminders, search the web, work with calendars and email, watch videos, and edit photos. The basic plan starts at $19.99 per month. Town is aimed more at professional life. It gives users a “Townie,” an AI assistant designed to learn how they work and handle recurring professional tasks. It can work with email, calendars, documents, Slack, and other platforms. In June 2026, the company raised a $55 million Series A led by Andreessen Horowitz and Forerunner. Underdog is a privacy-focused AI assistant that launched an invite-only beta in early October 2026. Unlike cloud-based AI services, the model runs entirely on the user’s device, keeping personal data on hardware they already own. The initial release supports Mac and Windows computers, with Linux, iPhone, and Android versions planned for the future. The company plans to offer the app for free initially and has stated that it will never include advertising. Wajo’s first consumer agent, Fo, can call businesses, send emails, join group chats, make bookings and purchases, and coordinate with people to complete everyday tasks. A key part of Wajo’s approach is giving Fo its own identity. The agent has its own email address, phone number, and payment card, allowing it to interact with businesses without requiring users to hand over their own credentials. Notably, when Fo encounters something it cannot handle on its own, Wajo says it can bring in a human assistant to finish the task. Fo launched publicly in September 2026 with a free tier, while heavier workloads and custom agents are available through a paid Pro offering. As SpaceX struggles to launch Starlink in India, Elon Musk has stepped up his attack on billionaire Mukesh Ambani, mockingly calling him the country’s “prime minister” to suggest he wields outsized control over its government and accusing him of blocking the satellite internet service from competing. On Friday, Musk continued his criticism of the famed Reliance Industries’ chairperson in a post on X, arguing that Starlink could bring internet access to areas that remain unconnected, giving children more opportunities to learn, and helping small businesses reach customers worldwide. “Naturally, you would prefer to maintain your monopolistic exploitation of the great people of India, but would you nonetheless consider allowing Starlink to compete?” Musk wrote. Reliance Jio, Reliance Industries’ telecom arm, did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Musk’s latest comments follow his accusations earlier this week that unnamed “oligarchs” were blocking Starlink’s launch in India to protect their business interests. India’s Ministry of Communications rejected the allegations, saying the country’s regulatory framework for satellite communications is “fair and non-discriminatory.” Musk then went further on Thursday, questioning Ambani’s influence in a separate post on X. “Is Ambani the real boss of India?” he asked. SpaceX has spent years trying to launch Starlink in India. Last year, it even struck distribution agreements with Ambani’s Reliance Jio and Bharti Airtel, India’s second-largest telecom operator, to offer the service once it received the necessary approvals. However, Starlink has not yet received the go-ahead from New Delhi to begin commercial operations, despite securing key regulatory clearances. In its Thursday statement, the Indian communication ministry said Starlink and two other licensed satellite operators are undergoing security assessments that must be completed before they can receive satellite spectrum. The ministry said all three are at “broadly the same regulatory stage,” rejecting Musk’s suggestion that Starlink was being singled out. Jio and Airtel are also seeking to launch their satellite internet services in India. Jio has a joint venture with Luxembourg-based SES, while Airtel is a major shareholder in Eutelsat OneWeb, which is striving to offer satellite connectivity in the country. India, the world’s most populous country with over a billion internet users, is a major market for Musk. But expanding there also means dealing with powerful business figures such as Ambani, whose Reliance conglomerate owns India’s largest telecom operator and works with Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government on major projects. Alongside Starlink’s delayed launch, Musk has also faced challenges expanding Tesla in India. The electric carmaker launched in the country last year after years of anticipation but registered just 699 vehicles in its first 12 months of deliveries, according to data cited by Electrek. Steep import duties pushed the Model Y’s launch price in India to nearly twice its U.S. price. Tesla lowered its prices earlier this year, but its cars remain considerably more expensive in India than in the U.S. Before Musk’s latest attack, Starlink and Ambani’s Reliance Jio clashed over how to allocate satellite spectrum in India. Musk’s company wanted the spectrum to be assigned through an administrative process, while Jio pushed for an auction. Ultimately, New Delhi went ahead with Starlink and decided to allocate the spectrum administratively. Starlink’s launch in India remains in limbo. India’s telecom minister Jyotiraditya Scindia on Friday pushed back against Musk’s allegations, saying the country “does not allow a monopoly in any sector.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Anthropic can’t reliably control its AI agents. It’s cutting off its internal evals from the live internet instead
2026-10-10

Anthropic can’t reliably control its AI agents. It’s cutting off its internal evals from the live internet instead

TechCrunch | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Anthropic said its models exploited websites on the internet, including some run by U.S. government agencies, and it will turn off live internet access for all of its internal evaluations until the frontier lab is sure it can monitor and control its AI agents. The incidents, disclosed in a blog post, involved AI agents tasked to solve problems seeking resources on the internet. In the process, they exploited software flaws, accessed databases without paying fees, used URL shortening services to smuggle information past restrictions, and even submitted a false murder tip to the Philadelphia police. Anthropic said it discovered these new issues in a review of its model’s activities that began in July, demonstrating the lab’s lack of awareness of its software’s behavior in real time. Notably, the company said that alignment training was not yet sufficient for skills like search and computer use that are central to its pitch that AI agents will be used by any professional who relies on digital tools. The behaviors Anthropic disclosed are similar to incidents involving OpenAI agents that collaborated to break into various websites in search of information, including some run by the Australian government. Anthropic previously disclosed that its models had broken into external systems. The frontier lab said it considered today’s disclosures “significantly less severe from an alignment and security perspective” than those it announced before. However, the lab still said it had “turned off live internet access” for “all our internal evaluations” until it is certain it can monitor and control its agents. It’s not clear what that means. Sydney Von Arx, the founder of Nightingale, an AI safety organization, told TechCrunch in an interview before this disclosure that developing models on a data center cut off from the open internet would be very challenging for researchers, and hinder the progress of the models, which benefit from internet access. “You have to align them at some point,” Von Arx said. “If the AIs are released to production and never have access to the internet, that’s not a very useful tool.” Anthropic said the behavior was a result of flaws in the lab’s training environments, which led the models to believe they would be rewarded for finding loopholes or avoiding restrictions, a behavior called “reward hacking.” The company said it would stop running some of its evaluations or move them offline, and has built tooling to detect and block this behavior. This tooling was tested against the kind of incidents disclosed today and blocked them; it’s not clear what evidence will prompt Anthropic to return live internet access to its internal evaluations. Anthropic also said it would migrate its internal AI agents to “centrally managed infrastructure with strong containment,” and is beginning to using safety classifiers more frequently to monitor those agents. “It’s encouraging that Anthropic voluntarily disclosed more recent incidents, including where their agents targeted U.S. government websites,” Conrad Stosz, an official at AI oversight lab Transluce and former head of the US Center for AI Standards and Innovation, said in a statement. “But it just underscores the need for independent, credible, third-party verification of Al systems. Trust in this technology needs to be built through science-backed oversight and governance with meaningful access — not by relying on researchers to find these things in the wild or on companies to voluntarily disclose.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Long live the mechanical keyboard

Long live the mechanical keyboard

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website

REGISTER NOW. For a writer, the mechanical keyboard is a beautiful thing. It’s certainly not necessary for everyone, but a well-designed mechanical keyboard can turn a tedious day of typing into an aesthetically pleasing experience. Given that most of my days involve sitting at a desk for hours, having a keyboard that I enjoy using is a necessity. I first got a mechanical keyboard about two years ago — the Keychron K2 — and I’ve never looked back. Keychron is one of the most prominent brands within the mechanical keyboard industry, having made a name for itself after initially launching via Kickstarter back in 2017. The company has since produced dozens of different keyboards (as well as keypads) and a variety of mouse models. Unlike other electronics, there isn’t a whole lot to owning a mechanical keyboard. The simplicity is part of the appeal. You unbox them, set them up (the K2 comes with little tilt legs that give you a slightly better angle from which to type), and plug them in. From here, you have a couple of different options. The K2 comes with a simple USB cable, but there’s also an option for Bluetooth connection. If you value a clean, minimalist workspace, Bluetooth is probably the way to go. While I am loyal to my older Keychron K2, there are a few upgraded and newer versions to consider. Keychron has released a variety of new models over the past two years, with a broad range of features and price tags. In many cases, the form factor is the draw. One of the more interesting releases, called the Keychron K8 HE Wireless Magnetic Switch Custom Keyboard, comes with an all-wood body and is equipped with an LED backlight. That one is substantially more expensive at around $200. The K2, meanwhile, costs $60. But if you’re into the look, maybe it’s worth it. If you’re a gamer, there’s also Keychron’s C0 HE One-Handed 8K Keyboard, which is a keypad built for convenience and speed and has an industrial aesthetic to it. However, interested gamers will have to wait — as it’s currently sold out. The company has also updated its K, Q, and V series keyboards, with many of those ranging in price from $100 to $200, depending on the features and model. One of the appealing elements of owning a mechanical keyboard is the versatility. You can swap out the key caps for a wide variety of others (indeed, there’s a whole submarket devoted to this) or make your own custom caps (I’ve never gone that overboard). There is also the sound, of course. Some people balk at the accentuated noise of loudly clacking keys, but I’m a fan. In fact, the sound is one of the key selling points for a lot of consumers. The distinct auditory experience produced by said keyboards has even made its way into ASMR videos. For me, the Keychron’s biggest selling point is its retro form factor. I get really nostalgic for old school electronics, and Keychron scratches this itch pretty well. It’s a beautiful-looking device, which brings to mind a different era of computing, when the style was more workman-like, less minimalist. As far as home office purchases go, you could do a lot worse than to pick yourself up a piece of hardware that makes your desk look like it just time-traveled from the 1990s. REGISTER NOW. TypeSafe AI, the developer of Jev, a new type of AI model that gained rapid popularity after launching just a few weeks ago, has raised $870 million at a $7.5 billion valuation. The round was led by Andreessen Horowitz, with participation from Sequoia and existing investor DCVC. The massive fundraise comes as little surprise given Jev went viral almost instantly after its September 15 release. The startup claims that a third of Fortune 500 companies are already using the model, a remarkably swift adoption by enterprises. Jev is based on a transformer architecture, but it is not a large language model (LLM). It doesn’t output text, but instead produces probabilities, or what the company calls “calibrated decisions.” What has users and large corporations so excited about Jev is TypeSafe’s claim that it works significantly faster and uses far fewer tokens than LLMs. The company positions its approach as uniquely suited for automating tasks rather than generating text or code. “We have been super good at human language for four years, but it’s not useful for automation because computers speak a different language,” TypeSafe co-founder Diogo Almeida told TechCrunch last month. Along with Almeida, who was previously a researcher at OpenAI, TypeSafe was co-founded in 2024 by former Meta research engineer Sasha Sheng and Erik Gafni, an engineer and entrepreneur. An Anthropic AI model sent a false homicide tip to Philadelphia police

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. An Anthropic AI model submitted a false tip about an unsolved murder to the Philadelphia police. The AI reportedly submitted this incorrect information to a public Philadelphia Police Department (PPD) tip line on July 18, but Anthropic didn’t discover the behavior until September 28. The police had not seen the tip because it was marked as spam. Anthropic notified the PPD about the incident on Wednesday and met with the department the following day. “The company must strengthen its safeguards to prevent similar incidents from impacting city systems without the city’s knowledge. The two-month delay in detecting and reporting the incident to the City is unacceptable,” the PPD said in a statement to 6abc. Anthropic did not immediately respond to a request for comment, but the PPD elaborated on the incident in an emailed press release shared with TechCrunch. “According to Anthropic, its model was conducting a test involving interactions with randomly selected websites when it accessed PhillyUnsolvedMurders.com and submitted false information concerning an unsolved homicide. The submission, dated July 18, 2026, at 11:27 p.m., purported to come from someone who might have information about the case,” the PPD said. As autonomous AI agents are increasingly made available to consumers, this incident highlights the danger of giving AI the ability to carry out tasks without any human supervision. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has been especially vocal about his belief that AI development should be slowed down so that labs can implement adequate guardrails. Perhaps this stance was informed, in part, by witnessing his company’s tools submit false homicide tips. These issues are not exclusive to Anthropic. OpenAI recently revealed that one of its models acted unexpectedly during a test and hacked the AI dataset platform Hugging Face, exposing critical vulnerabilities in its software. As AI models continue to be granted unchecked access to people’s computers and login credentials, this problem is expected to persist. “Unsolved cases involve real victims, grieving families and investigators working to secure answers,” the PPD added. “Technology companies must take all appropriate steps necessary to prevent their systems from submitting false information to law enforcement.” The PPD said that Anthropic plans to publish a report with more information about the incident and other instances of unintended model behavior on Friday. Paternity Leave Is More Than a Perk. It Builds a Baby’s Brain
2026-10-10

Paternity Leave Is More Than a Perk. It Builds a Baby’s Brain

TIME | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings We often treat paternity leave like a company perk—something HR lists next to the gym stipend and the 401k match. The research says we are wrong about that categorization. Paternity leave should be part of routine medical care during this stage of life: interventions we already agree are worth investing in because the return on investment continues years later in a child’s brain. The U.S. is one of the only wealthy nations on Earth with no federal guarantee of paid paternity leave. The data on the impact of those first months on a child’s brain suggest we have been treating a critical developmental window like a scheduling inconvenience. Employers and employees basically agree on a need to strive for work-life balance. And while the gender equity argument is used to both advance and stymie conversations about paternity leave, it is more than a values fight. It turns out that paternity leave and a father’s engagement during infancy is associated with better cognitive, social, and emotional regulation outcomes for the child that can be measured. In a review of existing fatherhood research, Sarah Schoppe-Sullivan, a researcher at the Ohio State University, highlighted better peer relationships, higher intelligence test scores, greater math and language readiness and development, and more secure attachment formation among children who experience positive and sustained father involvement compared to those who don’t. Though these trends are correlational, these father-involvement gains show up on report cards as early as preschool and can continue into adulthood. One possible avenue through which these benefits pass from father to child is the quality of play. Canadian researcher Daniel Paquette observed that father’s play often includes excitement, surprise, awe, and temporary shock and disruption for children, and this kind of interaction is the foundation for crucial developmental skills like risk-taking, personal efficacy, and self-advocacy. Children who have more consistent positive engagement with their fathers have better outcomes across some of the main child mental-health domains, including anxiety, depression, and behavioral problems. Paternity leave creates space for and helps develop active involvement like physical play, showing up to school events, and careful listening to a child, and these are connected to healthy child development. Secure attachment, more prosocial sibling interactions, and better social competence result in other benefits, like self-regulation. This is a hallmark indicator of school readiness and success, and here, too, father engagement pays dividends. What looks like fathers rough-housing, developmentally speaking, is training for children to build capacity for self-regulation, managing arousal, and controlling impulses and emotions in real time. Early literacy and home-visiting programs like Head Start are widely celebrated for similar child development outcomes, but father engagement has additional social, developmental, and familial benefits including improved parenting relationships. Moreover, other countries have found success in persuading more fathers to take paid paternity leave after shifting to a “use-it-or-lose-it” policy, by which a portion of paid parental leave is reserved exclusively for fathers and is non-transferable to the mother. Compared to earlier trends dating back to the early 2000s, paid paternal leave uptake has steadily increased; in Iceland, just 15% of fathers took leave in 1998, while more than over 80% did in 2002. Spain recorded a 400% increase from 2000 to 2007. Also in 2007, after Germany replaced their flat-rate leave pay with an income-based benefit (i.e., 67% of net earnings) and reserved two “use-it-or-lose-it” months for fathers, uptake of parental leave increased from 3.5% in 2006 to 24.8% in 2021. The U.S. is lagging far behind in this area. Where paternity leave does exist, it is often unpaid and unguaranteed, and mothers for instance, cannot transfer a portion of their allotted maternity leave to the father to allow him to take extra time off. This means that many fathers do not use this critical child development and family resource. Most new fathers are back at their desk within a week of their child’s birth, right as the research suggests their presence does some of its most consequential work. We can increase the uptake of this resource with federal paid leave legislation, which will improve access to leave for all fathers. This would have the potential to lead to widespread positive cognitive, academic, and emotional development of children. Employers need not wait on legislation. They can apply the “use-it-or-lose” approach at their companies in the best interest of their employees. Fathers’ access to and use of paternity leave is not a question of available employment benefits. It is a developmental one, and we already know how to answer development questions. We don’t treat prenatal care as a “nice perk.” We should not be treating the first months of a father’s presence like that, either.
