Are Firing Squad Executions Seeing a Revival in the U.S.?
2026-10-10

Are Firing Squad Executions Seeing a Revival in the U.S.?

TIME | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The United States Disciplinary Barracks, where Nidal Malik Hasan is being held on death row, is seen in Fort Leavenworth. Hasan, the U.S. Army Major who was convicted of killing 13 people and wounding 32 others during a mass shooting at Fort Hood in 2009, has been approved by President Donald Trump for execution by military firing squad. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Lethal injection has long been the prevailing method for carrying out the death penalty in the U.S. But supply-chain issues and a series of high-profile botched executions are increasingly causing officials around the country to weigh alternative options—including one that, until recently, had been all but entirely left in the past. Following the failed execution of Christa Pike in Tennessee last week—the second botched execution in the state this year using lethal injection—several Tennessee lawmakers have publicly called for the state to turn to other methods. “If we’re having this much trouble with this particular means of execution, we need to take it off the table and go to something that’s far more reliable and predictable,” Tennessee Senate Majority Leader Jack Johnson said while speaking on a local radio show on Monday. Republican Rep. Tim Burchett, who represents Tennessee's second congressional district in the House, similarly told local station WVLT last Friday that Tennessee should abandon lethal injection in favor of other methods that have “very little room for mistakes.” Tennessee House Speaker Cameron Sexton told The Associated Press in an interview published on Monday that “I don’t think there’s any method that’s off the table at this point.” Among the potential alternatives named by both Johnson and Burchett was execution by firing squad. The method has been used exceedingly rarely in the U.S.: between 1976 and 2024, just three people were put to death by firing squad in the country, all of them in Utah. But the Tennessee lawmakers aren’t the only ones who have recently suggested reviving the practice—and some state officials and President Donald Trump have already made moves to do just that. Trump on Monday approved the U.S. Army’s first execution of a soldier via firing squad since World War II, greenlighting the use of the method to put to death Nidal Hasan, a former U.S. Army major and psychiatrist who was convicted of killing 13 people in a mass shooting at Fort Hood in Texas in 2009, following a recommendation by Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. Hasan’s execution is scheduled to take place on Dec. 3 at Fort Hood and, Hegseth has announced, to be livestreamed. The approval comes months after Trump authorized the use of firing squads in federal executions in April. Both South Carolina and Idaho have also taken steps in recent months to either enable the use of firing squad executions or to carry them out. Last year, in the wake of a failed 2024 execution attempt using lethal injection in the state, South Carolina put three people to death by firing squad—doubling the number of such executions that had been carried out in the entire U.S. over the prior half-century. Idaho, meanwhile, enacted a law earlier this year that established the firing squad as the state’s primary method for carrying out the death penalty. That move, too, followed a high-profile botched execution via lethal injection in the state in 2024. The specific procedure for execution by firing squad varies across different states, but the general method remains the same, according to Austin Sarat, a professor of law and political science at Amherst College and the author of Gruesome Spectacles: Botched Executions and America’s Death Penalty,. He explains that traditionally, a person is strapped to a chair, a hood is placed over their head and a target is put on their heart, to highlight where the shooters should aim. Then, between three and five people with high-powered rifles will fire at the target. In some states, one rifle will be loaded with a blank bullet, so that the shooters do not know who fired the fatal shots. “When the signal is given, they shoot into the heart of the condemned,” Sarat says. Firing squad executions are currently permitted in just five states: Idaho, Mississippi, Oklahoma, South Carolina, and Utah. Idaho, under its recently enacted law, is the only state that uses the firing squad as its default method. The other states have designated execution by firing squad to be used as a fallback method if they are unable to carry out the death penalty by lethal injection, or as one of several methods that can be chosen by either corrections officials or inmates themselves to perform an execution. Utah in 2015 designated the firing squad as the alternative method to turn to if the state cannot, for instance, obtain the necessary drugs to perform an execution by lethal injection. Oklahoma permitted firing squads in 2011 as a method of last resort. Mississippi in 2022 gave discretion to corrections officials to choose among four methods, including the firing squad. And in South Carolina, the firing squad has since 2021 been one of multiple methods that death row inmates can choose for their means of execution, along with electrocution and lethal injection. If they do not choose a method, electrocution is used. Robin Maher, the executive director of the Death Penalty Information Center, a non-profit organization that provides analysis and research on capital punishment, tells TIME that at least one inmate appears to have opted for the firing squad in the state last year due to a lack of information provided regarding what other forms of capital punishment entail. “Prisoners who asked for information about the other two methods of execution, namely the electric chair and lethal injection, were not given complete answers to understand how those methods would be used, and so at least in one of those cases, the prisoners felt like the firing squad was the only method of execution they knew enough about to choose,” Maher says. Oklahoma became the first state to authorize lethal injection in 1977, as it sought more humane methods for capital punishment. From the late 1970s on, lethal injection was performed in Oklahoma and, later, other states using a three-drug protocol, explains Richard Levy, an anesthesiologist at Columbia University: one drug, sodium thiopental, to induce unconsciousness; a second paralytic drug to stop breathing; and potassium chloride to stop the heart from beating. In 2011, the company which manufactured sodium thiopental, Hospira, stopped manufacturing the drug, leaving states to revise their protocols for lethal injection. “Each state basically has adopted some variation on the theme,” Levy says in reference to the original three-drug protocol. He explains that the majority of states have since “gone to a single-drug approach,” a method he describes as “flawed and problematic.” Many states use a single dose of pentobarbital, which depresses the central nervous system. Levy explains that compared to thiopentol, pentobarbital doesn’t as effectively induce unconsciousness, “and probably doesn't reliably stop one from breathing.” Pike was given two doses of pentobarbital in her failed execution attempt before she was hospitalized; she complained that her arms were burning as the pentobarbital was administered, after several needles were unsuccessfully used to access a vein. Following the botched attempt, her lawyers believed she was brain dead on Monday, before she woke up on Tuesday. As of Thursday, Pike was “up and walking,” according to her lawyer. States have also struggled to obtain lethal injection drugs as a result of pharmaceutical companies’ opposition to supplying their products for executions. As a result, many states have substituted different drugs or opted for alternative execution methods. In South Carolina for instance, the state was unable to carry out executions for more than a decade after struggling to obtain lethal injection drugs, until 2021, when the state authorized firing squads and made electrocution its default method. In 2023, the governor’s office revealed that it had reached out to more than 1,300 contacts for execution drugs before finally obtaining pentobarbital. The state then resumed executions by lethal injection, but in March of 2025, a death row inmate chose to be executed by firing squad over fears of prolonged suffering related to lethal injection, becoming the first person to be executed by a firing squad in the state’s history. After it was unable to obtain sodium thiopental or pentobarbital, Oklahoma in 2014 switched to using midazolam, which has since been used in multiple executions that were considered botched. One Oklahoma execution using midazolam that year lasted 45 minutes. Republican Utah state Rep. Paul Ray, who sponsored the bill that reinstated firing squads in his state, cited difficulty obtaining the necessary drugs as a reason for the move when speaking with The Los Angeles Times in 2015. “We found out last year that there we had no ability to purchase more drugs. So looking down the road, we had to come up with a backup plan,” Ray told the outlet. “We decided to make it automatic to go to the firing squad.” Federal officials have given voice to similar reasoning. Before Trump authorized the use of firing squads for federal executions, the Department of Justice released a report in April which said that switching to “manners of execution that are currently provided for by the law of certain states … will help ensure the Department is prepared to carry out lawful executions even if a specific drug is unavailable.” The report also acknowledged “supply chain challenges that limit the availability of lethal injection drugs.” As those issues have complicated efforts to carry out lethal injection executions in recent years, botched attempts like the one in Pike’s case have also contributed to officials’ decisions to shift toward alternative methods. Lethal injection, by far the most common method of the death penalty in the U.S, also comes with the highest rate of botched attempts, according to research performed by Sarat, the Amherst college professor. In one comprehensive study that examined executions between 1890 and 2010, Sarat found that just over 7% of executions by lethal injection were botched. In a subsequent study looking at executions performed between 2010 and 2020, he found that the rate was over 8%. “It is certain. It is quick. And it brings justice for the victims and their families in a more expeditious manner than other types,” said Idaho state Rep. Bruce Skaug, who sponsored the bill that established the firing squad as the state’s default method. Historically, in the scant instances when firing squad executions have been carried out in the U.S, they’ve rarely—if ever—been botched, according to Sarat’s study. Out of 34 executions by firing squad between 1890 and 2010, he found, not one went significantly awry. In one of the three firing squad executions performed in South Carolina last year, though, an autopsy report concluded that the firing squad missed the heart of the victim, indicating that he did not immediately die and may have endured suffering. Sarat argues that firing squads, though involved in far fewer botched executions, remain a “brutal” form of capital punishment that “was abandoned because it didn't meet the evolving standards of decency of the society.” “Since the end of the 19th century, the United States has been on a quest to find a method of execution that would be safe, reliable, and humane. The firing squad has never played a big role in that story,” Sarat tells TIME. “It hasn't played a big role in that story because it is a brutal way to kill someone, and its brutality is very obvious.” Other forms of capital punishment have also received consideration in recent years in the search for potential alternatives to lethal injection. The DOJ’s report from April directed federal prisons to expand their methods of execution to include, along with firing squads, gas asphyxiation and electrocution. Alabama in 2024 became the first state to execute a death row inmate using nitrogen gas, and four other states have adopted the practice since. In 2025, 19 bills in 14 states were introduced to alter or augment execution methods, according to the Death Penalty Information Center. Despite the moves to revive largely bygone execution methods or implement new ones, however, Sarat emphasizes that capital punishment is overall trending down. The death penalty is currently outlawed in 23 states, while 10 others have not carried out an execution in the last decade, according to the Death Penalty Information Center. An additional four states have not had an execution in the last five years. “The United States is on the road to abolition … The death penalty is dying,” Sarat says. “I don't think that either what Trump has done about the firing squad or Christa Pike's failed execution, in and of itself, is going to make a huge dent in America's death penalty.”

Trump Taps Katie Zacharia to Replace Karoline Leavitt as White House Press Secretary

Trump Taps Katie Zacharia to Replace Karoline Leavitt as White House Press Secretary

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump has selected conservative commentator Katie Zacharia to serve as the next White House press secretary, he announced Friday. Zacharia will replace Karoline Leavitt, who stepped down in August, in the position. Zacharia currently works as a communications adviser at Trump Media and Technology Group, the company that runs Trump’s social media platform Truth Social. She previously worked at the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) as a spokesperson for former Secretary Kristi Noem, and appears frequently on conservative media including Fox News and Newsmax. Zacharia wrote on X that being selected as White House press secretary was “the honor of a lifetime” and vowed to “[communicate] to the American people the major wins that this Administration is making every single day.” It is not clear when Zacharia will start her new role. The Trump Administration, which often refers to itself as the “most transparent” in history, has not held a formal press briefing in 36 days, since Vice President J.D. Vance took to the briefing room in early September. In a post on Truth Social announcing his selection, Trump praised Zacharia for “an unblemished record of support” and being “a loyal and brilliant supporter, right from the beginning.” “She is, likewise, a devoted fan and member of MAGA!” Trump continued. “I am confident that Katie will deliver strong results for our Country as the next White House Press Secretary, as she has the respect of, and will work fantastically with, the Media, the American People, and my Administration.” The New York Times first reported that Zacharia had been tapped by the White House. Zacharia, who currently lives in California and graduated from Pepperdine Caruso School of Law, has also worked as a general counsel for almost three years at Fix California, a conservative non-profit founded by Ric Grenell, who served as Acting Director of National Intelligence during Trump’s first Administration. The organization works to promote a conservative agenda around the state. In June, Zacharia was appointed to the advisory board of NovaRed Mining Inc, a Canadian mining company based in Vancouver, according to a company announcement. Just a week before Zacharia’s appointment, Noem also joined the advisory board. Leavitt, who became the youngest White House press secretary in history at the age of 27, this week joined Fox News as an on-air contributor, the network announced on Monday. She is the third of Trump’s former press secretaries to join the network. She begins on Nov. 1. Trump announced Leavitt’s departure through a Truth Social post in which he said that she “will now be one of my top outside advisors, and an influential voice within the Republican Party, as we work to defy History, and conclusively win the Midterm Elections.” Leavitt responded with a lengthy post on X in which she thanked the President and the White House and said that she would remain “a vocal advocate for MAGA and the Republican Party. ” Leavitt gave birth to her second child, Viviana, in May. She went on maternity leave that month and later announced that she would be stepping down from her role to spend more time with her family. Trump’s time in office has been characterized by a contentious relationship with the media, which he has repeatedly attacked and derided while serving as President. Leavitt echoed that attitude during her time behind the podium, becoming known for her acerbic remarks and quick deflections during press briefings. Zacharia will enter the White House during a fight over the media’s access to the President. Trump on Sept. 18 banned POLITICO, CNN, and MS NOW from the White House grounds, sparking an ongoing legal battle. Though the outlets’ access to the White House has been restored after a federal judge issued a temporary restraining order against the ban, Trump has since restricted POLITICO and CNN from boarding Air Force One, preventing them from carrying out their White House pool duties and drawing further blowback. Zacharia would also be entering the White House at a time when Trump is facing record-low approval ratings over his handling of the war in Iran, which has contributed to rising inflation in the U.S., and as the fast-approaching November midterms threaten Republicans’ control over both chambers of Congress.

Update your preferences in Account Settings Korean dramas are famously chaste compared to Western TV series. Though there are some exceptions, most K-dramas shy away from featuring even passionate kissing, let alone full-on sex scenes. But Take Charge of My Heart, Netflix’s latest Korean-language romance, adds a jolt, making  “skinship,” or the physical intimacy between two people, a central part of its premise. In the supernatural series, TV scriptwriter Na Bo-bae (When the Phone Rings’ Chae Soo-bin) has electrical currents coursing through her body, making it so she will zap anyone she touches with bare hands. Because of the condition, Bo-bae has never dated. The lack of real-world experience is hindering her latest TV drama script—a romance about a chaebol heiress and her bodyguard. Meanwhile, chaebol heir Baek Ho-rang (Trigger’s Kim Young-kwang) has an artificial heart that is failing. When Ho-rang and Bo-bae happen to be in the hospital at the same time, Ho-rang’s doctor discovers that physical proximity to Bo-bae charges Ho-rang’s artificial heart. Even more shocking to Bo-bae, she can touch Ho-rang without anything happening to him. As Ho-rang waits for a surgery for a new artificial heart in three months, the two strike a deal: Bo-bae will move into Ho-rang’s massive house, and act as his portable charger, and Ho-rang will go out on dates with Bo-bae as research for her script. Of course, what begins as a mutually beneficial arrangement becomes something deeper when the two fall in love. Will corporate machinations from Ho-rang’s aunt and cousin, Choi Do-yoon (Idol I’s Jung Jae-kwang), or an unexpected change in Bo-bae’s powers keep them apart? Here’s everything that happens in the Take Charge of My Heart’s finale. Take Charge of My Heart plays fast and loose with its plot logic, which is to say that the series is not overly concerned with consistency or science when it comes to characterizing Na Bo-bae’s powers. Our protagonist was hit by lightning as a child. She survived the incident, but hasn’t been able to touch others without electrocuting them ever since—until Ho-rang. While Ho-rang’s resistance to Bo-bae’s powers could be artificial heart-related, the series also implies that it has more to do with fate. The two are meant to be together. Much of Bo-bae and Ho-rang’s relationship is encouraged by the necessity of their skin-to-skin contact. While Bo-bae can charge Ho-rang’s artificial heart by being within 10 meters of him, her charge-up is even more effective when the two hold hands, hug, or kiss. Though they don’t kiss as part of their contract, their hand-holding and embraces help both Bo-bae and Ho-rang realize that they have feelings for one another. While we learn early on in the series that they were on their way to see Ho-rang, the full context of the tragedy doesn’t become clear until the finale.. At the time, Ho-rang was in the hospital because of his heart condition, and was being visited by his grandmother, aunt, uncle, and cousin. Upset at constantly being overshadowed by his cousin, Choi Do-yoon called Ho-rang’s parents and lied to them, saying something was wrong with their son and that they needed to come quickly. In their rush, Ho-rang’s parents got into a fatal car crash. Years later, Do-yoon is still traumatized by this accident, and is still sick of being treated as the screw-up of the family. Do-yoon has not given up on being the successor to the TS Group, the family chaebol. But he knows he needs to get rid of the much more competent Ho-rang to do so. Knowing that Bo-bae is Ho-rang’s priority, Do-yoon kidnaps her on the night of the TS Group’s 30th anniversary celebration. When Bo-bae doesn’t arrive, Ho-rang is anxious but continues on with his speech. However, because Do-yoon also spiked his drink, Ho-rang’s artificial heart battery is losing power fast. He collapses on stage, his heart no longer functioning. Meanwhile, Bo-bae uses her electric powers to escape from the temple Do-yoon’s hired goons have locked her in. She and Jae-ho rush to the party and arrive shortly after Ho-rang has collapsed. A desperate Bo-bae gives Ho-rang CPR, and sends a massive electrical current into his body. Ho-rang’s heart starts beating again, but Bo-bae collapses in the effort. When she wakes up again, Ho-rang’s doctor tells Bo-bae that she can’t touch Ho-rang again without endangering his life. He had surgery to get a new artificial heart, and Bo-bae’s electrical touch could stop it. Chae Soo-bin as Na Bo-bae and Kim Young-kwang as Baek Ho-rang Park Ji sun—Netflix A heartbroken Bo-bae flees the city to go to her parents’ house in Dae-jeon, leaving a still-recovering Ho-rang alone. She soon discovers that her touch no longer shocks people. Her powers have temporarily short-circuited before, after large discharges of energy, but they always come back. Though she is able to touch others without hurting them, she worries that her electricity could return at any time. Bo-bae, who is worried about Ho-rang’s health, is hesitant at first. But Ho-rang quickly wins over her parents by helping them with their muscat grape jam business. He convinces Bo-bae to take a chance on them, and they return to Seoul to oust Do-yoon from the TS Group. The ousting doesn’t take much effort, as Do-yoon soon sabotages his own role in the company. She meets Bo-bae, who quickly wins her over with her kind heart. Bo-bae’s career also gets back on track. Her new drama, The Bodyguard in My Heart, begins filming. Bo-bae’s best friend, Cho Seo-jeong (Kim Ah-young), is the director. Ji Eun-ho (Study Group’s Cha Woo-min), Bo-bae’s actor friend who confessed his unrequited feelings to Bo-bae earlier in the season, films a cameo in the series. Ever the supportive boyfriend, Ho-rang comes to the filming. At the time of the series’ ending, Bo-bae’s powers have not returned. Carbon Accounting May Be the Most Important Climate Fight You've Never Heard About
2026-10-09

Carbon Accounting May Be the Most Important Climate Fight You've Never Heard About

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Who is responsible for each ton of carbon emissions? How that question is answered will shape everything from climate regulations to corporate energy purchasing decisions. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings I’ve found a surefire way to mess with the good vibes during my on-the-record conversations with CEOs and senior executives: bring up carbon accounting. In an instant, a conversation that felt open and transparent moments prior can turn into guarded talking points or simply go quiet. Carbon accounting may be the wonkiest topic in the already wonky world of climate change and emissions reduction. And few areas inspire more passionate debate and infighting than the rules for measuring and attributing them. But underneath the jargon lies a high-stakes question: who is responsible for each ton of carbon emissions? How that question is answered over the coming months and years will shape everything from climate regulations to corporate energy purchasing decisions. A report by the Aspen Institute and shared exclusively with me ahead of its Oct. 9 release offers a rare window into that fight. It draws on a multi-day summit this February that brought together the field’s key players—including academics, NGOs, and industry voices—to debate the path forward on how best to account for carbon emissions. I attended the closed-door summit having agreed to report on the discussion without identifying individual participants. It concluded in a ceasefire of sorts with promises to work together even as the core debates remain unresolved. Making sense of the climate and energy economy. By signing up you are agreeing to our Terms of Service and Privacy Policy. To understand the carbon accounting landscape, you need to understand the system at the center of the debate. For more than two decades, one approach, known as the Greenhouse Gas Protocol (GHGP), has flourished and become the de facto global standard. Under GHGP’s approach, emissions are divided into scopes: Scope 1 for direct emissions from a company’s operations, Scope 2 from a company’s purchased electricity, heat, and cooling, and Scope 3 for value chain emissions. The GHGP approach has driven a variety of guidance documents and informed regulation and certifications issued by other entities. But, as with any such initiative, GHGP is not perfect. And a growing number of stakeholders—particularly businesses—have called for changes of varying scope and scale. The resulting fault lines in the carbon accounting debate are many. There are technical questions like over what time scale (hourly vs. annual) companies must match their electricity use with their clean energy purchases. And there are questions about the degree to which carbon accounting relies on estimates rather than true measurements. But, perhaps more than anything else, there is a debate about responsibility: in accounting for emissions, who should really be held responsible? The technical matters are divisive on their own. The philosophical questions cut to the core of the climate challenge. The weather in Aspen was unseasonably warm. That didn’t keep the seminar room from turning chilly at times. While no one in Aspen said that the GHGP approach is perfect, perspectives on what to change and by how much varied widely. Defenders of the current system say that Scope 2 and Scope 3 reporting has motivated voluntary corporate action. Scope 2 rules, in particular, give companies a clear way to claim credit for the clean electricity they buy, and that predictability has helped fuel a boom in long-term renewable power contracts, known as power purchase agreements. And predictability matters. While in theory it may be possible to design a better system, the GHGP approach is entrenched, and it could take decades for a new system to earn such widespread adoption. The critiques are multifold. The GHGP approach allows for “double-counting.” In other words, multiple companies end up accounting for the same emissions under different scopes. Think of steel in a building. The steel producer, the construction firm, and the building owner are all technically responsible for the steel emissions. A primary alternative approach, which originated in academia and now has a coalition of industry players advocating for it, accounts for emissions using “e-ledgers.” Every unit of emissions is accounted for once, placed on a ledger, and then transferred as a product moves through the world. It’s an elegant approach with real advantages. It would incentivize companies to focus on decarbonizing their own operations and give them high-quality emissions data to use in procurement. But some in the room in Aspen noted that it would be difficult to implement. It only really works if every single player on the value chain participates. The sharpest divide, however, concerns emissions from using a product. Under GHGP’s Scope 3, an oil company counts the emissions from every gallon of gasoline its customers burn. That is the primary driver of its enormous carbon footprint. An e-ledger works differently, with emissions traveling with a product and responsibility belonging to whoever does the burning. That leaves consumers with greater accountability even if they have little influence over the broader system. The e-ledger approach emerged from the corners of academia in a series of highly publicized articles beginning in 2021. And, in 2025, a coalition of companies including ExxonMobil, BlackRock, and others backed the launch of a business coalition to advocate for an e-ledger approach. At the same time, the call for change comes amid the retrenchment of climate from its front and center position in the public zeitgeist, making some companies less willing to commit the resources necessary to fully embrace the work required by GHGP. GHGP has responded, hiring a new CEO and instituting a string of reforms. Given the stakes and the players involved, it’s no surprise that the conversations in Aspen were heated at times. And yet the summit ended with a detente of sorts, as participants agreed to work together to find common ground. Indeed, some participants noted that e-ledgers can help with tracking a product’s carbon footprint, while the broader GHGP rules should apply for a whole company. What happens next in this wonky corner of the climate community concerns more people than you might expect. It will shape regulations—including climate disclosure rules in California and the European Union—and affect where businesses target their climate initiatives. Perhaps most importantly, it will affect how we answer the question of who is responsible for future emissions.

Update your preferences in Account Settings Law enforcement officers investigate after ICE agents shot and wounded a man in the Bronx in New York on, Oct. 8, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A day after a federal immigration agent shot a man in New York City, the New York Police Department released footage from one of their officers responding to the shooting. The release came amid a growing outcry over the shooting of 28-year-old Oscar Belgal, which has prompted calls for Immigration and Customs Enforcement to immediately release body cam footage of the ICE agent involved in the shooting and for ICE to end their operations in New York City altogether. The NYPD’s video, which did not capture the shooting itself, showed two police officers showing up to a scene where a man was sitting on the ground, with one person holding on his neck to avoid further injury. One ICE officer, who was wearing a neon construction vest, an ICE badge and holding an assault rifle, was making a phone call and did not talk to the NYPD officers. While responding to the scene, one NYPD officer asked if the victim should be transferred to a hospital, but ICE officers did not respond to his question. “Are we putting him in [the police vehicle] or what?” The police officer asked. Multiple police officers eventually lifted the person on the ground onto the police vehicle which drove to the nearest hospital. Shortly after the shooting, social media videos taken from different angles on the scene also showed that the back window of a silver SUV was completely shattered and the front window was pierced by at least one bullet. A child was seen taken away from the shooting scene by a plainclothes officer. Earlier on Friday, Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said the ICE officer involved in the shooting had been equipped with a body camera and that there was footage relating to the incident. “As the investigation moves forward, as I said, we’ll release the information,” he said. TIME has reached out to ICE regarding when the body camera footage from the ICE officer will be released. ICE’s policy requires its officers to activate cameras during routine enforcement activities, including while making arrests, executing search warrants and responding to emergencies. In an event when a serious injury or a death occurred during the operation, a panel of ICE internal leaders will review the footage and decide whether footage should be released. According to ICE’s internal policies released in February 2025, this process should take within 72 hours of recording, or as soon as operationally feasible But the release isn’t always guaranteed. According to the policies, ICE can withhold footage from body cameras if there are “specific and compelling circumstances justifying an objection to public release that cannot be resolved by redaction or other means.” The decision would lie in the hands of the ICE director. The released footage from ICE will also have ICE officers’ faces and identifiable information redacted to protect its officers’ privacy, according to the policy. The agency spent tens of millions this year in purchasing and equipping its officers with body-worn cameras, following a pair of deadly shootings in Biddeford, Maine and in Houston over the summer. The ICE officers involved in both shootings did not wear body cameras when conducting operations.

The story about her daughter crashing the simulator says a lot about how she worked. Hamilton wanted a safeguard against starting a prelaunch program during flight; she was told the astronauts were too well trained to make that mistake. Then Lovell did it on Apollo 8, and her team had to help recover. Anyone who's been told "the user won't do that" should recognize that argument. She took the possibility of failure seriously, and the priority handling in her team's Apollo 11 software meant an overloaded computer could keep doing the work needed for the landing. Imagine watching that happen during the actual descent, knowing the recovery mechanisms your team built were being put to the test. I'm glad she lived long enough to receive so much recognition for that work. Rest in peace. There may be more comments in this discussion. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Here’s How—and Where—It Could All Go Wrong for Democrats
2026-10-10

Here’s How—and Where—It Could All Go Wrong for Democrats

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter that was glad to be reminded that we need to find a rich person to pay off our $100,000 in credit card debt if we’re ever going to get that ambassadorship. So … any takers? The air had a crisp autumnal smell of politics to it this week. The Surge has pulled its giant foam finger saying “DEBATES” out of storage, because friends, it is debate season. Wonder what Ken Paxton thinks privately about anything? You’ll probably learn about it through leaked audio soon. We take a look at the looming Senate Democratic leadership race (there won’t be one) and observe Trump taking a warning shot at two of his top political advisers. Let’s begin, though, with a look at the Senate map, and the state it might all come down to. In January, after Democratic former Rep. Mary Peltola announced she’d run for Senate in Alaska, Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer gave a series of interviews about how he’d landed “the last piece of the puzzle” toward retaking the Senate majority. The path he laid out ran through Alaska, Ohio, North Carolina, and Maine. “We couldn’t have better candidates,” he told Semafor. While some new opportunities for Senate Democrats have since entered the fray—Iowa, Texas, and Kansas—it’s still those four states that represent the core pathway. And by our reckoning, it’s the bluest of those four states, Maine, that represents Democrats’ biggest hurdle. Republicans at this point have effectively ceded the North Carolina Senate race to Democrat Roy Cooper, with both the Senate Republicans’ super PAC and GOP candidate Michael Whatley’s campaign itself pausing their advertising. (Note: Cooper does still have to win more real-life votes than Whatley in the election for this concession to become official.) Alaska and Ohio are hardly in Democrats’ column already. But Ohio Democratic Senate candidate Sherrod Brown has consistently posted polling leads against a weak opponent in Sen. Jon Husted. And just this week, Sabato’s Crystal Ball, one of the top election forecasters, shifted Alaska from a “toss-up” race to “leans Democratic.” Which brings us to Maine. Oh, Maine. RealClearPolitics’ polling average shows Democratic candidate Troy Jackson with a 1-point lead over Sen. Susan Collins—but with significant variation between individual polls, and in a state where Collins tends to outperform her polls. The list of reasons why Democrats should take this seat without much struggle is limitless. Briefly, though: It’s a blue state in a blue year where the Republican president is heinously unpopular. Trump has picked a stupid trade war with a country abutting Maine, with which it conducts significant amounts of trade for daily goods. Mainers need heating oil, and heating oil costs are through the roof. But there are two reasons why the race is on a knife’s edge. The first is that Jackson is not running against a generic, hapless Republican, he’s running against Susan fucking Collins. The second is the long tail of Democrats’ candidate drama. When Schumer gave those interviews in January about how “we couldn’t have better candidates,” he was referring to his personal recruit, Gov. Janet Mills. But, as one member of Graham Platner’s team told us when they were being criticized for not having properly vetted him, Schumer’s vetting of Mills missed that she was 78 years old and didn’t want to run. The rest of the story we’re all familiar with: Platner built a movement, and then imploded personally, leaving Jackson as the warm body to fill the ballot line. As our colleague Ian Prasad Philbrick wrote in an excellent piece about the race this week, “now the party is stuck, trying to win an election Platner shaped with a candidate who has to shoulder much of his predecessor’s baggage but lacks many of his talents.” It would just make too much sense, and be so dang Democratty, to fall just short of Senate control by failing to beat Susan Collins for the 900th time, with the best chance they’ve ever had to do so. Too much sense, too neatly choreographed. So maybe Democrats will get it done after all. Feeling a little under the weather? That’s because you’ve got debate fever, suffering reader. We’re at that point in the calendar where people across the country (well, us, since it’s our job) need a C-SPAN multi-view feature to keep up with all of the jabs; the digs. Do any of these debates matter? As much as we’d love to play it cool and laugh off the notion that anything matters, well, they sometimes do! Republicans lost a Senate seat in Indiana in 2012 after their candidate discussed in a debate what qualifies as “legitimate rape.”  South Carolina Sen. Darline Graham quite nearly lost her primary a couple of months ago after saying she didn’t know anything about foreign policy. And if we’re remembering correctly, a 2024 debate between Joe Biden and Donald Trump had a substantial effect on that race. We didn’t notice any blow-ups of such magnitude in the debates we watched this week. Our main takeaway from watching Senate debates in Georgia, Maine, and Michigan was how they reinforced the state of each race. Sen. Jon Ossoff is cruising to reelection in Georgia against Rep. Mike Collins, and it’s so easy to see why: With perfect poise and discipline, he delivered banger after banger against his opponent. (For example: “If John Lewis were marching today from Selma to Montgomery, Mike Collins would be grabbing a billy club.” The kid’s a killer.) In Maine—a viewing experience that shaped our perspective of this race in the lead entry—Troy Jackson had all of the advantages in the world but struggled to get his answers out clearly, while Collins, always perfectly prepared, was at no point caught off guard. In Michigan, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed was, as ever, perfectly eloquent, charismatic, and cocky, but took repeated hits against some of his more left-wing positions, such as single-payer healthcare. Republican candidate Mike Rogers, meanwhile, consistently race-baited El-Sayed. In an illustrative example, Rogers described his opponent as “bought off by Hamas, Hezbollah, al-Qaida.” But since no one in these debates parsed the meaning of “rape,” declared that they don’t know anything about the world, or fell asleep during their answers—things of that nature—we’re not expecting any major shifts from these. You may have heard chatter about Democratic leadership in the next Senate. You may even have heard murmurings that Chuck Schumer, who lost standing in the party in last year’s government funding battles, might have his work cut out to remain as leader. This is nonsense. So long as Schumer runs for leader again, he will be the leader. If Schumer actually does flip a net of four Senate seats and retake the majority, it would not just ensure he gets another term as leader—it would be his finest political feat, period. If he comes up just short, he’ll be fine, as it’s not like some other leader would have found better recruits in states like Alaska, Texas, Iowa, or Ohio. And if the night goes catastrophically for Democrats and they pick up no seats, or even lose a couple, well, that just lowers the number of senators Schumer would need to win another term—to 23, 24, 25. Schumer does indeed have that many friends within the Senate Democratic caucus. Now, a good question for next year is whether Schumer, 75, chooses to take the opportunity time has presented him to step down after the next Congress, following 12 years as leader, or run for reelection and spend two years dragging the caucus through the messy politics of his own primary. But again: a good question for next year. ·     Trump chastised two of his top outside political hands, Chris LaCivita and James Blair, on social media after a story ran about how they were doing some consulting for a pro-Russian political party in the Balkans, threatening to fire them if he discovers anything untoward. Trump’s reaction makes no sense at first. Since when does he care about conflicts of interest or people in his orbit getting paid by pro-Russian factions? What’s happening here is that Trump put LaCivita and Blair in charge of his midterm operation, the midterm operation is not going well, and he’s mad at them. He’s reminding them who their meal ticket is. ·      In other Trump posting news this week: He has nicknamed South Carolina Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews “Annie Trannie.” Yes, oh yes, it is newsworthy that the president of the United States is using this slur. Zooming out, though, it’s even more newsworthy that he’s turning his attention to Andrews at all. He posted the slur while reiterating his endorsement of Andrews’ opponent, Sen. Darline Graham. Trump also plans to hold a campaign rally in South Carolina before the midterms. And the top Senate Republican super PAC is now spending $5 million in the state to shore up Graham’s position. Just a reminder: This is a statewide race in a state where Trump beat Harris by 18 points. If the midterm polls are overstating Democrats’ position heading into November, well, the GOP’s internal polls must be, too. ·      We don’t want to single out retiring North Carolina Sen. Thom Tillis. There’s a bad incentive system out there in which those who at least wrestle with whether to confirm a bad nominee get more criticism than those who don’t apply critical thinking at all. But Tillis, in promoting his new book, has described his deciding vote to confirm Pete Hegseth as Defense secretary as his “biggest regret.” He added, though, that “based on the data I had at the time, I’d do the same thing again.” What other “data” did he need? Hegseth was a weekend television co-host who had published a book the previous year titled Here Are the Ways I’d Be an Asshole If I Ran the Military (paraphrasing). Prior to that, he had mismanaged running a charity, had been married 47 times (estimating), and faced serious accusations about his drinking and treatment of women. And this man was nominated to lead the American military. It wasn’t a tough decision. ·      The Atlantic reported that Trump was having his people draw up plans for attacks on Iran before the midterms, and shortly thereafter, Trump posted that “we will not be attacking Iran at any time prior to the Midterm Elections to be held in the United States on November 3rd.” This is an interesting way for the commander-in-chief to tie his own hands on the use of force for a set period of time. It also overtly concedes that the war timeline is being dictated by the political calendar. Last, it reinforces that he probably does have plans for after the midterms. ·      We’re just tickled by how every day or so, some new audio of Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton at a private event leaks. You can set your watch to it. That doesn’t mean that what he’s caught saying is particularly interesting, though. When he said that every Republican candidate’s numbers dropped after the GOP midterm convention, that was simply true. When he said that it’s “the war, it’s gas prices, it’s diesel” dragging his candidacy: Ding, ding, ding! When he said that “making some reasonable cuts to mandatory spending—you’re gonna have to do it”—well, yes, that’s the Republican Party position and one that the GOP legislated on just last year with its cuts to Medicaid and SNAP. Did he say anything truly new at this fundraiser, like that he thinks James Talarico is a pretty good guy or that he enjoys wearing dresses when mowing the lawn? The Antidote to Our Anti-Democratic Supreme Court

The Antidote to Our Anti-Democratic Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
America needs bold ideas to break the Supreme Court’s grip on democracy. Enter … the shadow court. Please enable javascript to get your Slate Plus feeds. Donald Trump brags about his boundless freedom while the same Supreme Court that handed him that power has curtailed everyone else’s. Confidence in the court has cratered to historic lows, and waiting for a justice to retire is not actually a plan. Could a “shadow court”––a body set up to critique and even to rival SCOTUS—rescue democracy? In her new book, The Shadow Court: Rescuing Democracy from the Supreme Court, Fordham law professor Julie Suk argues that the court isn’t just making some bad decisions—it’s working exactly as designed. Drawing on constitutional courts in democracies around the world that rebuilt the rule of law after falling to fascism and authoritarianism, Suk outlines a bold institutional answer: a shadow court that would issue advisory opinions on the same issues the Supreme Court decides, giving lawmakers and the public a concrete alternative to judicial supremacy. Dahlia and Julie also discuss how America’s legal and constitutional systems continue to fail women (also by design), from the stalled Equal Rights Amendment to Justice Alito’s claim that women’s reliance on Roe was merely “abstract”—and why the path to real change might run through state ratifying conventions, rather than state and federal institutions that have failed to reform themselves. There’s a Reason Pete Hegseth Chose the Fort Hood Shooter for His Grotesque Publicity Stunt
2026-10-09

There’s a Reason Pete Hegseth Chose the Fort Hood Shooter for His Grotesque Publicity Stunt

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Execution by firing squad is an especially brutal form of the death penalty. A promise to publicize the execution to the masses reeks of a bygone era of American disgrace, amplifying that brutality. On Thursday, the Pentagon announced a plan to do just that when it carries out the execution of Nidal Hasan. Hasan was a member of the military when he murdered 13 soldiers at Fort Hood in Texas. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has scheduled Hasan’s execution for Dec. 3. “Of course we’re going to do it at Fort Hood,” Hegseth crowed. “And do it on a Thursday and we’re going to do it at 1 o’clock—which is when he did it—and it’s going to be soldiers with rifles executing the firing squad.” The plan to livestream the proceedings is unprecedented in the United States. As the New York Times explains, “Releasing video of executions is most often associated with extremist militant groups like the Islamic State. …There are few countries that hold public executions.” The most prominent of those countries is Iran. Amnesty International is right to say that “executions in public serve no legitimate purpose and only increase the cruel, inhuman and degrading nature of this punishment.” That is precisely the point of the Trump administration’s plan for Hasan’s execution. Hegseth says that he wants the American public to watch because they need to understand the “serious consequences” for Hasan’s crime. But livestreaming Hasan’s execution will not be about transparency or a desire to help the American public better understand what the government does in our name. Instead, it will be the kind of spectacle of cruelty that has become a staple in the administration’s use of power, like having masked Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents roaming American streets or proudly posting videos of the American military killing people in boats off the coast of Venezuela. Public executions were once a staple of American life. From the beginning of the republic, they were carried out in town squares across the country. They were supposed to be moments of public education when citizens could witness justice being done and in which the condemned would deliver a “gallows speech,” acknowledging the sinfulness of the crime and repenting for what they had done. Parents and teachers would often bring children along to teach them a lesson. The events would sometimes feature a choir, a preacher giving an “execution sermon” and the condemned, who was typically a man being hanged, giving a long speech to the crowd. Occasionally executioners even achieved a kind of celebrity status. Arkansas’ George Maledon was so well-known that he was dubbed the “Prince of Hangmen.” Throughout Maledon’s career as a hangman, Arkansas newspapers regularly reported his identity and his role in executions. But by 1936 when Kentucky carried out the last public execution, all that changed. Their educative purpose had given way to a circus-like atmosphere with thousands of people assembling to witness a gruesome event, often tinged with the brutal white supremacy of the Jim Crow south. The New York Times recalls the event, which included an audience of 20,000 people, this way: Kentucky was the only state still conducting public hangings, a fate reserved for what was viewed by Kentucky’s citizens as the most heinous crime: rape. The condemned was a young black man named Rainey Bethea. He had been convicted of raping an elderly white woman, a wealthy widow who was found choked to death in her bedroom. Bethea’s execution carried with it the taint of a lynching as had previous public executions. And these executions seemed simply uncivilized, the sort of thing that decent people would not want to see. Once new execution technologies like the electric chair became the preferred execution method, putting people to death in the town square was no longer feasible. Scholars have claimed that there is something very different about the visceral experience of witnessing the state putting someone to death and the experience of watching that happen on television or a video feed. Wendy Lesser argues that such a viewing experience would be voyeuristic, allowing viewers to see from a safe distance something that is itself shameful. What Hegseth intends to do would indeed be shameful. It would be the equivalent of the government producing and distributing a snuff film, a film that captures a real killing and uses it to appeal to viewers’ sadistic impulses. Unlike lethal injection, the effects of which are often hidden behind a medicalized facade, the brutality of the firing squad is exactly what Hegseth wants Americans to see. Hasan will be seated in a chair with a hood placed over his head with a paper heart affixed to his chest. A group of anonymous men, each carrying identical rifles, will stand behind a wall cut with gun ports and on command shoot their high-powered rifles. The damage done to Hasan’s body will be devastating. That’s why even Vice President J.D. Vance says he won’t watch it. As though a nod to those racist public spectacles of yore, Hasan’s Muslim background almost certainly explains why Hegseth is so determined that he be the first to endure such a punishment in such a public way. In the end, though, a brutal public execution will not bring honor to the American military or to this country. It will unfortunately only confirm the widespread view that our country has lost its mind and abandoned any pretense to decency in its dealings with its citizens, or those of other nations. All of us will pay the price for Hegseth’s unspeakably vile publicity stunt. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. The Saving Grace of Trump’s Revenge Tour
2026-10-09

The Saving Grace of Trump’s Revenge Tour

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Just eight days into Donald Trump’s first term, the legal expert Benjamin Wittes coined a phrase that would come to describe many of the president’s actions: “malevolence tempered by incompetence.” Wittes’ idea was that Trump’s inattention to detail and ignorance of how government works often undermine his malign aims. More than nine years later, the phrase still applies—this time to Trump’s efforts to use the power of the federal government to punish his political enemies. A president determined to weaponize his office is, obviously, a dangerous perversion of how American presidents should behave. Yet two new investigations reveal both the malevolence and the incompetence of this president’s ongoing revenge tour. The first effort reflects one of Trump’s main preoccupations: his 2020 election defeat. After Trump claimed fraud and egged on the crowd that sacked the U.S. Capitol on Jan. 6, some of his own officials turned against him. One of them was Cassidy Hutchinson, a former aide to Trump’s then–chief of staff, who testified to a House committee investigating the attack that Trump wanted magnetometers near the White House removed to let potentially armed rioters join the crowd. On Thursday, we learned that Trump’s Justice Department is reportedly trying to convince a grand jury in D.C. to charge Hutchinson with a crime or crimes, which could include lying to Congress. It’s not yet clear what prosecutors think Hutchinson lied about, but threatening a former subordinate with jail time in an attempt to rewrite the history of your coup attempt is as nefarious as it gets. And while it’s certainly possible that the grand jury will indict Hutchinson, several of Trump’s other efforts to turn the justice system against his enemies have fallen flat. Grand juries in Washington have refused to criminally charge Democratic lawmakers who urged service members to disobey illegal orders, a protester accused of throwing a sandwich at a federal immigration agent, and several others. Still other indictments—against New York Attorney General Letitia James and former FBI Director James Comey—have fallen apart under judges’ scrutiny. The DOJ itself has also struggled to find pretexts on which to hang indictments of other Trump foes and is hemorrhaging staff as career prosecutors balk at taking part in nakedly political investigations. Unlike in his first term, Trump is surrounded today by true believers who are earnestly trying to realize his revenge fantasies. But the law keeps thwarting them, and the Hutchinson case could become the latest example. Still, the president has shown a dogged willingness to try, try again. His second effort to go after a political foe this week is actually a redo of an earlier one. On Friday, Trump announced a White House committee to investigate Lisa Cook, a Federal Reserve governor, over allegations of mortgage fraud leveled by one of the president’s own appointees. The committee, which is stocked with Trump appointees, is set to hold a hearing next month about whether Cook should lose her job. Trump, who has accused the Fed of political bias, seems to be gunning for Cook in part because she’s a Joe Biden appointee. But he’s also targeting her now because his previous attempt to fire her flopped. In a rare adverse ruling for the president, the Supreme Court temporarily blocked Trump from removing Cook in June, saying she hadn’t had sufficient opportunity to contest the claims against her (she denies them). But a majority of the justices also signaled that they don’t like the idea of presidents removing Fed officials on a whim because the central bank’s independence is key to setting sound monetary policy. So even though Trump’s new committee looks like a kangaroo court, Cook’s future is still very much in doubt. One reason the president’s revenge tour keeps flailing is that it’s largely a product of his delusions, which keep running smack into the brick wall of reality. But the possibility that Trump will fail to nail both of his latest targets doesn’t mean what he’s doing is benign. The mere fact of being investigated can exact a toll in stress and legal fees, and even watching Trump fall short could discourage others from standing up to him. We’re also less than halfway through Trump’s second term, and if his first is any indication, things could get rockier toward the end. If Democrats retake Congress next month, it may leave Trump more isolated and inclined to lash out. Buckle up! Slate was an early adopter of the listicle, that now-familiar internet format. Over the years, the magazine has ranked everything from Super Bowl halftime shows and horrible wedding songs to pre-YouTube viral hits and Declaration of Independence signatures. In honor of the magazine’s 30th birthday, my colleagues have compiled a list of lists to keep you company over the long weekend: All 339 Olympic events: The next Olympics is still a couple years away, but it’s not too early to start brushing up on your knowledge of the Summer Games. Before the torch is lit in Los Angeles in 2028, make sure to consult Slate’s list of every Olympic event, ranked from worst to best, compiled by Mike Canter. (For those who prefer the Winter Games, Justin Peters has you covered, with rankings of the best and worst sports—and the events ranked by the likelihood of a dog winning.) The 40 greatest stand-alone TV episodes of all time: If you want to watch some TV this weekend but aren’t sure where to start, Slate has you covered. In this list, our writers recommend the best episodes that you can dive into right away—without having to commit to the whole series. It’s a great way to sample a new show. All 349 “murmurs” in the Twilight saga: In this very scientific investigation, Rachelle Hampton ranked and charted all the times Stephenie Meyer used the word murmur in her YA vampire series. Come for the many murmured quotes, stay for some top-notch literary analysis. The three things you need to know about politics this week: Slate senior politics writer Jim Newell is here to give you a sanity check. Kathy Hochul Says New York Needs to Close the “Cornell Seven” Loophole. That’s False.
2026-10-09

Kathy Hochul Says New York Needs to Close the “Cornell Seven” Loophole. That’s False.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. A civil lawsuit alleging binge drinking, rampant drugging, and gang rape at Cornell has sparked a firestorm on social media and front-page headlines. The incident occurred two years ago. Since then, the university has investigated the “Cornell Seven”—the seven frat brothers directly involved—and imposed discipline ranging from expulsion to compulsory essay writing. For many, this should never have been a campus matter but one for our criminal justice system. There has been particular ire for the reported criminal law “loophole” that the district attorney claimed tied his hands in this case, preventing initial charges. This is a red herring. Even with the current law, prosecutors had everything they needed to charge the alleged perpetrators in this case and potentially win conviction. Allow me to explain. Politicians have reacted predictably to this case with claims that criminal law is not tough enough, and prosecutors need more tools. Slamming Tompkins County District Attorney Matthew Van Houten for declining to charge the men, Gov. Kathy Hochul urged the Legislature to “close the loophole” that keeps prosecutors from convicting people who have sex with the intoxicated and the insufficiently participatory. I recall a time not too long ago when progressives and campus feminists agitated for reducing mass incarceration and the reach of America’s racially biased policing and punishment system. But such institutional concerns evaporate in the face of stories that invoke justifiable outrage against the nauseating misogyny of fraternity culture but also invite gendered ideas about women’s sexual vulnerability, ruination, and squeamishness over sexual impropriety. The impulse to avoid acknowledging that victims chose any part of a harmful sexual encounter produces overbroad criminal laws that leave little room for female sexual agency. To be clear, there are no intoxication or consent “loopholes” in New York sexual assault law. New York’s regime prohibits forcing others into sex (“rape”), imposing sex against their will (“sexual misconduct”), and having sex with “physically helpless” people (“rape”). If members of the Cornell Seven used force or threats to compel sex, or even just proceeded without Jane Doe’s consent, they committed a crime, regardless of whether she or they were drunk. Contrary to the DA’s analysis, New York law does not require unequivocal verbal refusal for sex to be nonconsensual. Perusing the DA’s justification for declining prosecution, one readily sees facts that could support charges. Jane Doe, surrounded by larger men, felt intimidated and unable to leave. She swatted hands away and said “no”; she hid under covers and became “9 out of 10” intoxicated. The DA could have brought a criminal case. We need not create a new crime of sex without an “affirmative expression of consent”—one that would render consensual, even desired, but “yes”-less sex criminal—to get at the wrongdoing alleged here. The same can be said about intoxication. Sex with a person intoxicated to the point of helplessness is already a crime—in fact, first-degree rape. A drunk but conscious person can be “physically helpless” if intoxication leaves them “unable to communicate unwillingness”—a factual determination New York courts have left to the jury. Jane Doe had been drinking heavily when she arrived at the frat house, but she was able to consider and agree to the threesome. The epistemic mismatch between her idea of what would transpire and the frat brothers’ is truly tragic. Doe had reportedly intended to hook up with Matthew Ingalls and agreed for Jonathan Newell to join in, “excited” by the prospect that they might be gay. Far from fulfilling a Heated Rivalry fantasy with male friends who “had her back,” she entered a dystopia of manosphere misogyny. Based on Doe’s legal complaint, she became the victim of a fraternity culture bent on getting one over on women as an act of toxic homosocial bonding, as evidenced by a group chat that attempted to reduce her to “free pussy.” Indeed, research finds that college men often have sex they don’t want because of pressure to conform to masculinist norms. Doe’s friends-with-benefits scenario shattered, according to the complaint, as the situation grew increasingly coercive, nonconsensual, and she approached or crossed the threshold into intoxicated helplessness. Unfortunately, the current conversation, including the call to broaden criminal law, accepts the dyad where a woman is either a complicit “slut” or a helpless, and later ruined, victim. The men’s defenders insist that Doe is the true deviant who eagerly agreed to kinky—even painfully kinky—sex, then lied about it because she was shunned from a party. A strain of “consent to some is consent to all” even runs through the DA’s explanation for his decision not to charge. But the outraged rejoinder has not generally emphasized that women can be sexual agents who agree to some activity and are assaulted in the same encounter, or that unwise and unpleasant but consensual sex is different from, yet can cross into, criminally wrongful sex. Instead, the lawsuit and many internet pundits deny that Doe had the capacity to make choices at all. She moved through the world requiring protection from drinking, from going to meet a guy she was interested in, and from agreeing to unconventional sex. So eager are online feminists to spare Doe the stigma of the girl in the threesome—a stigma we should be fighting, not reinforcing—that they insist that, despite Doe’s reasonable rationale for agreeing to it, she could not have been thinking straight. Doe herself described the binary, recalling reading an account that she was “gang raped” and thinking, “That’s the first time I had ever heard the narrative change that I wasn’t a whore and that I was assaulted.” So what exactly do Hochul and company mean by “closing the intoxication loophole”? Do they want to criminalize sex with someone who is intoxicated but appears lucid and able to agree, as Doe initially did? Given that neither a defendant’s own drunkenness nor their reasonable belief that the other consented is a defense, it behooves us to be clear about how drunk is too drunk, because that is the only line that matters. If both parties are sufficiently intoxicated when they have sex, each is a victim and a perpetrator. That both believe the sex was mutually desired is immaterial. Making intoxicated but not incapacitated sex a crime would grant prosecutors enormous authority, given how often drinking and sexual activity go together, especially among the young. Which groups will constitute the “bad guys” against whom prosecutors wield their newly minted power to prosecute drunk sex—and will they look like the Cornell Seven? Broadening criminal law to encompass widespread sexual behavior should happen only after forthright public debate. It should not be done in a viral reactive moment and couched as merely closing a loophole that doesn’t even exist. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Two characters open up a world of typosquatting opportunities in Chromium browsers
2026-10-10

Two characters open up a world of typosquatting opportunities in Chromium browsers

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Attackers abusing rare Cyrillic and Latin letters to impersonate popular websites Researchers say two characters available to typosquatters and phisherfolk can trick Chromium browsers into displaying lookalike URLs as genuine web addresses. Wangling a domain name to look an awful lot like that of a popular website is nothing new. We’ve all encountered phishing sites such as macrosoft[.]com and applle[.]com before in our daily struggles against spam. However, as browsers mature, new characters are always being made available for use. This opens up new opportunities for those whose languages contain characters/homoglyphs that aren’t ASCII-compliant, but it also introduces new ways for attackers to abuse display logic quirks in programs like Chrome and Edge. According to Ian Muscat and Leanne Briffa of Have I Been Squatted, there are still characters available to cyber-imposters that can reliably fool web users into trusting URLs that they certainly should not. The latest glyphs bypassing browser safety checks allow tricksters to run websites from a clearly fake domain name (when displayed in Punycode), although they appear just like the real deal in Unicode. The characters of note, in this case, are ө, which is found in various Cyrillic languages such as Kazakh, Mongolian, and Tatar, and the Latin K with hook, ƙ, used in Hausa and Karai-karai. Both are visually similar to the letters e/o, i, and k, respectively, and allowed the researchers to register 20 lookalike domain names. Below are the URLs’ Punycode equivalents – how browsers should display them safely: You can try them out; they’re registered by Have I Been Squatted and are safe to visit. They take you to research demo pages set up by the researchers, and each page explains how exactly they bypass browser protections. Crucially, they all bypass the key security measures deployed by Chromium-based browsers. Browsers deploy two main defense layers. The first is a set of seven sequential checks run by Chromium’s SafeToDisplayAsUnicode function, which check for a range of common spoofing methods. Vendors began introducing these checks in 2017 after researcher Xudong Zheng registered аррӏе.com – a domain consisting of all-Cyrillic characters. Chromium’s checks, which factor in a hardcoded list of known Cyrillic characters known to be used in place of Latin letters, can be seen as "all or nothing." Built to detect all-Cyrillic strings, the measure only takes action against domain names if every character in the string is on its hardcoded list. If one character is missing, the check is bypassed. Characters such as ө, ї, and ү, are known as “breakers,” as these are not present in the lookalike list, as of Chrome 154  (released to stable channel on September 22). Failing any of the seven display checks tells the browser that the characters are unsafe to display as Unicode and to instead display the Punycode, revealing just how dissimilar they are to the genuine domains they try to imitate. The second measure browsers take is to compare the domain name against a list of popular websites to see if it is trying to imitate one of them. In Chromium, these checks are handled by the GetSimilarTopDomain() function. This step converts a supplied domain name into a "skeleton," stripping its characters of diacritics, such as accents (ó becomes o), and checking the skeleton URL against a hardcoded list of popular websites. Chromium checks against almost 8,500, and if the skeleton matches any of them, then it displays Punycode. However, the Latin K with hook, ƙ, does not have an accent, and is added to the skeleton as a Latin k with an added combining mark, bypassing the security check. In this case, the skeleton is formed as: [o k ‘ t a . c o r n]. Likewise, with аррӏө.com, the Cyrillic barred o retains its bar in the skeleton as a combining mark, meaning it does not match the genuine Apple domain. Its skeleton is formed as: [a p p l o - . c o r n]. Browsers are always working to stymie these tools of imposterment. Chrome 148 put an end to attackers being able to use ҏ and ӿ as breakers to imitate their Latin lookalikes, for example. The two main security checks are not the only lines of defense. Chromium also deploys warnings at the time of navigation called Safety Tips. An example domain that would bypass the two main checks is ínstagarm[.]com. The inflection on the beginning character is removed and what’s left is essentially the real Instagram domain with two letters swapped. The Instagarm domain does not match any of the hardcoded sites, nor does it contain any banned characters. In this case, Chrome will display one of two popups, asking the user if they meant to visit the genuine domain, warning that the current direction of travel appears fake. However, there are limits to this extra security layer. The warnings only trigger when an imitation domain is an exact-character match, a one-edit match, or a match with an adjacent swap from the genuine URL. Two or more changes, like with аррӏө[.]com, which has all-Cyrillic characters preceding the ".com," will not trigger these warnings. The warnings will also not trigger with domains consisting of fewer than five characters. Domains such as oƙta.com, which is only one edit from the real Okta, may not trigger defenses because of the one-edit rule and the fact that it is only four characters. Safety Tips also checks the skeleton against the sites users visit regularly, not just the hardcoded list of trusted sites. Frequently visited sites may trigger the same warnings, but for users who do not have a comprehensive visit history, the defense layer may fail. The defenses described here only apply to browsers. Email clients are even less picky with imitation domain strings. Websites like Gmail displayed each of the 20 domains HIBS fed the clients, which were a mix of lookalikes and genuine internationalized domain names (IDNs), as an attacker would want it to, all in Unicode. Outlook Web showed all 20 as Punycode, even the harmless ones, suggesting neither apply the right checks to properly inform users. To quantify the scale of the issue, the researchers looked at ICANN’s list of every .com domain. There are around 167 million of them, approximately 733,000 of which are IDNs, those that contain non-ASCII characters and are stored in Punycode form. The researchers took each of the IDNs and swapped their non-ASCII characters for ASCII equivalents to determine how many matches there were. They found around 162,000 pairs – IDNs that resemble plain ASCII domains. It should be said that this does not mean there are circa 162,000 typosquatted domains, just that many yield lookalikes. Some may be registered by businesses for defensive purposes; others may be owned by the same business that wants to operate multiple websites catering to different languages. However, organizations should be aware of the means available to typosquatters, and monitor registered domains accordingly. ® Source attribution matters to 79% of respondents – handy for a company licensing its Q&A archive to model builders The first version of Microsoft-Decision-1 is based on Qwen3.5-9B, but the next will sport homegrown tech, Redmond reassures PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters But don't worry, it's totally safe because the agents and models will (probably) be running locally.
