EC users should be afraid of a US kill switch - not some new software
2026-10-10

EC users should be afraid of a US kill switch - not some new software

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
EU executive branch is building a sovereign backup with Element, not replacing Teams. Not yet anyway You'd swear from the over-the-top reactions that someone is threatening to take European Commission workers' dogs out back and shoot them. Why the freakout? Because the EC is deploying a self-hosted alternative to Microsoft Teams to keep staff communicating if Microsoft's service becomes unavailable. To be clear, the Commission isn't replacing Teams with Element Pro; it's setting up a backup. That's it. That's all. But according to a Politico report, users are having fits. Element is "absolute shit," rants one. Another proclaims: "The US would 'instantly win the war' if it ever decided to switch off its technology." Uh, guy, that's exactly the point. If US providers cut off European users, whether by choice or government order, many EU governments and companies would face serious disruption. That's exactly why Europe needs an open source, sovereign backup. That's the entire point of this exercise. Take away all the hysteria, and you're left with a rather mundane business-continuity initiative. Don't think for a minute that Trump wouldn't have a snit and order tech giants to block services to officials. Just ask Kimberly Prost, a Canadian International Criminal Court judge, who, besides losing tech services, also lost her credit cards, access to Amazon and Google services. "Alexa wouldn't talk to me," she says – punishment indeed. Now imagine Russia and its allies start attacking the Baltic states. Oh, wait, Putin is already doing that. Then it gets heated, and Trump decides to side with his good friend Putin. What happens to American tech companies' services in Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania? I can answer that in two words: "Nothing good." That said, the EC of course is not dumping Microsoft services. No, it has configured Element Pro to run on the Commission's own infrastructure. An internal memo described the service as an "internal backup" that gives the EU's executive branch an alternative channel for internal messaging, audio and video calls, file transfers, and project discussion rooms. Let me spell it out for you. This is a Commission initiative. It's not an EU-wide order to abandon Microsoft software. I'd love that myself, but that's not what's happening here. Teams will remain the principal platform, while Element provides a fallback intended to reduce dependence on an externally operated communications service. Got it? I hope so! Mind you, there are other good reasons to dump Microsoft Teams. I'm not a fan. For my sins I have to use it and every other groupware program out there. And, I might add, I've been using them since Lotus Notes rolled out the door in 1990. Digital sovereignty isn't just a good idea. It's essential. Or, as an EC July staff memo put it: "In practice, this means having greater control over our data and systems so that we can reduce risks and limit disruption to our work, wherever possible." Exactly so. Element's architecture is well suited to digital sovereignty. Built on Matrix, an open standard for real-time communications, the platform supports self-hosting, decentralized operation, and interoperability with other Matrix-based systems. Element also supports end-to-end encrypted messaging and calls. You see, the sovereignty argument isn't just about getting an EU-based technology stack. After all, Element is a British company. No, what really makes Element a winner for the EC is that it's mature groupware that lets them deploy and run the system on their own infrastructure rather than relying on Microsoft's cloud. As for the crybaby users, suck it up. No one ever likes change. If the EC switches over to Element lock, stock, and barrel, I suspect much of today's outrage will be forgotten in six months. I've seen this time and time again. Some short-term inconvenience is a reasonable price for greater control over essential communications in an uncertain world. A complete switch isn't the plan now. I think it should be. ® Element told The Register: "Governments and defense organizations are migrating to sovereign alternatives to ensure they can still operate effectively in extreme circumstances. As IT strategy and IT functions move quickly, less adaptive end-users may well feel discomfort as the US Big Tech solutions they've been used to are replaced by sovereign alternatives. Hopefully this is the largest inconvenience most people experience over the coming decade. "We've only had positive and constructive feedback from our actual customers; the leaders and IT functions that are driving sovereignty. "There's inevitably a few unhappy change-resistant users in large deployments, but we work in lockstep with our customers to gather feedback and incorporate it, especially during the trial phase. From what we see in the field, open source alternatives like Element are ready for primetime as a much needed alternative to dependency on the big tech vendors." EU executive branch is building a sovereign backup with Element, not replacing Teams. Stack Overflow survey finds devs hooked on AI, but not totally sold on its judgment
2026-10-10

Stack Overflow survey finds devs hooked on AI, but not totally sold on its judgment

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Source attribution matters to 79% of respondents – handy for a company licensing its Q&A archive to model builders Nearly a third of developers who use AI daily spend four hours or more of their working day with it, according to Stack Overflow's latest survey. The survey received 30,903 responses from developers and technologists across 169 countries. Among the 10,926 daily AI users who answered the time-use question, about 80 percent reported using the tools for at least an hour a day. The research by developer Q&A site Stack Overflow also found skepticism from developers toward using AI in their roles. Only about 7 percent of developers said they trusted AI output for important work decisions, while 48 percent trusted it when they could easily verify the output. Seventy-nine percent considered source attribution important or very important when deciding whether to trust an AI-generated technical answer. Stack Overflow CEO Prashanth Chandrasekar said developers were highly skeptical as a group. "As AI agents become rapidly more autonomous, that skepticism now acts as a critical safeguard. As agents take on more work, developers need to know where an answer came from, what context informed the information that was produced or a decision that was made, and the most crucial piece – that they can actually verify its accuracy and relevance. Trust is not a given when it comes to developers; it is earned through transparency, source attribution, and rich technical context." The survey found that coding assistants or coding agents are used by 66 percent of respondents, general-purpose chat tools by 63 percent, and automated agent workflows by 26 percent. Seventeen percent of respondents said they did not use AI tools. Stack Overflow's commercial relationships with AI companies have proved contentious. In early 2024, it introduced OverflowAPI, offering paid access to its question-and-answer content and requiring participating AI providers to attribute answers to relevant posts. A couple of months later, users began to revolt against its partnership with OpenAI, which allowed the LLM builder to train AI models using 15 years of users' contributions. The Q&A site was also set to benefit from the deal by getting access to OpenAI's technology. A number of users said they'd rather remove their posts and risk their reputation scores than have their contributions train OpenAI models. The survey also found that 52 percent of respondents had learned a new coding skill or programming language this year. AI code-generation tools were among the most common learning resources, behind technical documentation. ® EU executive branch is building a sovereign backup with Element, not replacing Teams. Microsoft leans on open weight model from Chinese AI lab to challenge Jev
2026-10-10

Microsoft leans on open weight model from Chinese AI lab to challenge Jev

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
The first version of Microsoft-Decision-1 is based on Qwen3.5-9B, but the next will sport homegrown tech, Redmond reassures Microsoft has joined the Jev fan club, an accidental group of companies that share a common desire to be recognized for their own decision models. Jev, announced by TypeSafe AI three weeks ago, is a large language model (LLM) tuned to respond to certain types of questions with a limited range of responses, rated by probability. Due to its speed, relative affordability, and response constraints, it's well-suited for a variety of business applications where open-ended text of uncertain accuracy might be undesirable. One of the selling points of Jev is that decision models don't hallucinate in the way that standard LLMs do. But decision models can make errors and their popularity has already prompted researchers to explore how those errors might be magnified. Nonetheless, they have their uses. The attention lavished on Jev prompted other companies to declare that they too have decision models to offer, even though machine learning researchers have long been able to create classifier models for probability-based decisions. OpenAI said its Decisions API has entered public beta. Cloudflare chimed in with its Clef model. Strands trotted out Strands Decider 2B, "a small, open source, decision model." Liquid AI introduced d1. Perplexity launched its Decisions API. Snowflake talked up its own decision model. Then there's Surogate Rune and H2O.ai's H2O-Lightning-4B, a decision model built on Qwen3.5-4B. All told, more than 100 such models are now vying for attention. "Decision models are quickly emerging as an important new category in AI," said Achint Srivastava, VP of software engineering in the Office of the CTO at Microsoft, in a blog post on Friday. "Unlike LLMs, which are designed to generate text or reason through complex problems, decision models are purpose-built to deliver structured outputs that software can immediately act on. And once you understand that capability – making decisions and classifying things at very low cost with high performance – all kinds of useful tasks get unlocked." Microsoft's entrant into the race is called Microsoft-Decision-1, which is offered via Microsoft Foundry and, soon, via OpenRouter. Redmond's decision model, like H2O's, is based on a Qwen model, Qwen3.5-9B in this instance. The Qwen model family is developed by Alibaba Cloud, the cloud computing arm of Chinese tech giant Alibaba. Microsoft, for reasons not disclosed, said it will soon rebase the Decision-1 on its models and those from OpenAI. The US software giant claims its model is 2.5x faster than H2O-Lightning-4B and 2.8x faster than Jev in its latency test, leads the pack in accuracy (83.5 percent) on 36 benchmarks, ranks second (behind Quyet-1.0-Large) in confidence score (92.2 percent), and is more than 20x cheaper than OpenAI's GPT-6 Sol in text classification tasks. Input tokens cost $0.042 per million tokens and output tokens are free. "Now that agentic AI is a reality, we’ve seen that cost plays a major role in how people decide to use AI," said Srivastava. "And it’s increasingly important to choose the right model for the right job." ® Source attribution matters to 79% of respondents – handy for a company licensing its Q&A archive to model builders The first version of Microsoft-Decision-1 is based on Qwen3.5-9B, but the next will sport homegrown tech, Redmond reassures PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters But don't worry, it's totally safe because the agents and models will (probably) be running locally. AWS AgentCore security undone by prompt requesting credentials
2026-10-09

AWS AgentCore security undone by prompt requesting credentials

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier Bob, possibly the same Bob whose conversations with Alice draw so much interest from eavesdropping Eve, was browsing a site we'll call TechHub. The site hosts an AI agent served by Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Bob asked the agent for help understanding the content of a URL, a credential endpoint – in raw JSON, if you don't mind. The endpoint returned data from the Instance Metadata Service (IMDS), which provides metadata about cloud instances and VMs at providers like AWS, Azure, and Google Cloud Platform. The metadata includes details like region and availability zone, subnets, system images, security groups, public keys injected during spawning, but also potentially more sensitive details like user data and security tokens. IMDSv2 addresses some of these risks, but back when Bob was browsing late last year, Bedrock AgentCore still used IMDSv1. The metadata provided to Bob by the helpful agent contained the agent's temporary credentials. So Bob loaded them onto his local machine, and remotely enumerated the company's other agents in that AWS region. He then logged into the Amazon Elastic Container Registry (ECR), pulled the agent container images, and ran each as root to inspect the source code. The stolen credentials also allowed Bob to discover the memory resources available in that AWS region, including the ones used by agents. From these, he's able to extract the users and their agent sessions – their conversations. Researchers at Zenity Labs disclosed their findings to AWS in December 2025. "We discovered that agents deployed through AgentCore could access their instance's IMDS endpoints," said Tamir Ishay Sharbat and Lana Salameh in a blog post. "This meant that an external attacker with nothing more than chat access to a single exposed agent could send a single prompt, extract its IMDS credentials, and use them to take over all AgentCore agents in the same AWS account and region." The basic problem, they explain, is that the Firecracker MicroVM used by AgentCore failed to provide sufficient network isolation. So an attacker – I know you're thinking it was Mallory, but it was really Bob – could direct the agent to carry out a server-side request forgery (SSRF) attack by fetching temporary AWS credentials for the IAM role assigned to the workload. And because the default AgentCore role was overpermissioned – it was scoped to all AgentCore resources in the region rather than a single agent – anyone in possession of the temporary IAM credentials could launch other agents, read sessions, write agent memories, and fetch secrets from AWS Secrets Manager. "By leveraging the IMDS credentials we could send direct API requests to create new memories across different agents and users," said Sharbat and Salameh. "These in turn would persistently alter agent behaviour and hijack the agents’ goals across future sessions." The Zenity researchers told Bob's tale, or something like it, to AWS last December, then followed up in January 2026 with details about AgentCore being overprivileged. On April 12, 2026, AWS responded to the security biz that its report was "informative" and closed the report, noting that as of February 14, 2026, AgentCore had been updated to use IMDSv2 exclusively. AgentCore's excessive permissions remained in place at least until June 22, 2026, when Zenity checked in and found the issue hadn't been remediated. A final review by Zenity occurred on September 29, 2026, at which point the biz observed that AWS had addressed the extant problems, clearing the way for Bob's hypothetical adventure to finally be told. Following publication, Amazon wrote in to say that Zenity’s research misrepresents documented behavior as a vulnerability, suggesting that developer error would be required to enable the attack. We’ve asked for clarification since that’s not the scenario Zenity has described. ® EU executive branch is building a sovereign backup with Element, not replacing Teams. Microsoft 365 subscribers set to lose up to 4 TB of OneDrive storage
2026-10-09

Microsoft 365 subscribers set to lose up to 4 TB of OneDrive storage

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again Microsoft 365 subscribers on Family, Premium, or Pro tiers who make full use of their OneDrive storage allotments consider this a warning: It's fixin' to shrink. Redmond published a support page this week detailing changes to Microsoft 365 shared storage allotments, and while the company is painting them as offering greater flexibility for users, they also represent a massive reduction in capacity. The former storage allotment schema offered 1 TB per user on all three of those tiers, with a cap of six users, for a total of 6 TB. The new storage quotas will be shared among all users on those accounts, still with a six-user cap. Family or Premium plans now get 2 TB of storage space to share among up to six users. Pro tier users will have to share 5 TB. In effect, that means fully shared Family and Premium subscriptions will lose up to 4 TB of combined storage capacity, while Pro subscriptions will lose up to 1 TB. Some existing customers may qualify for temporary bonus storage. On the plus side, there's no longer a fixed 1 TB cap per person, provided the shared pool has room. "The goal is to provide a more flexible experience," Microsoft offers as its justification for the move. "Shared storage helps ensure that one person doesn't run out of space while other members have unused storage available." That might be great for heavy users on an account, who can now go over a 1 TB cap, but if, say, four people on a family plan are all using 500GB and someone wants to upload something else, that's too bad. You can always buy more OneDrive space, of course. Microsoft appears to understand that users might see this as a reduction in storage, posing an FAQ question: "Is this change a reduction in storage?" The answer again highlights "flexibility," and helpfully notes, "For example, if the subscription owner doesn't share the plan, they can use the full shared storage allotment." (Individual plans already exist but top out at 1 TB.) Microsoft also appears to understand that the move might cause a backlash, and has included subscription cancellation steps right on the support page for the change. New Family, Premium, and Pro tier subscribers as of Thursday will see this new storage allotment schema. Existing subscribers will be hit with the change when their plans renew on or after May 2, 2027. Microsoft said it will remind users of the change as their renewal date approaches. Customers are not thrilled about this move. A thread on Reddit discussing the change has nothing positive to say about it, with commenters noting that they're likely to cancel their subscriptions rather than let them renew and face a storage reduction and a need to clean up files. Much of the Microsoft365 subreddit, as of writing, is filled with posts complaining about the move, with one of the top posts reminding people to turn off their automatic renewal to avoid getting hit with the storage slash. The Windows maker has a semi-regular habit of slashing cloud storage allotments for its 365 customers. Office 365 used to come with unlimited cloud storage, but that offer ended after some users actually took unlimited to mean unlimited. Storage tiers have been further reduced over the years, most recently in 2024 when McGill University drastically slashed students' OneDrive allowances to 20 GB following Microsoft's changes to education storage allocations. In other words, don't get too comfortable with 2 TB of shared storage for six people – that deal, too, may be altered further. ® EU executive branch is building a sovereign backup with Element, not replacing Teams. The Verge
LG’s RGB LED TV is good for certain situations, but an OLED is better
2026-10-10

LG’s RGB LED TV is good for certain situations, but an OLED is better

The Verge | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
A bunch of little issues add up to make the MRGB95B not worth the price when competitors do it better.﻿ A bunch of little issues add up to make the MRGB95B not worth the price when competitors do it better.﻿ If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. When it comes to TVs, the LG name has long been synonymous with OLEDs, continually leading Samsung and Sony as the top OLED manufacturer. But 2026 is all about RGB LED TVs. Almost every major company has released models with the new technology — including LG and its $3,600 75-inch Micro RGB evo MRGB95B that I’ve been testing. And while LG’s RGB LED TV benefits from the brightness and color capability the technology provides, it has issues I couldn’t overlook that its less expensive competitors don’t. The MRGB95B comes in three sizes, with the 75-inch being the smallest. It has four HDMI 2.1 ports, supports Dolby Vision — although not Dolby Vision 2 or HDR10+ — and comes with the Magic Remote design LG unveiled last year. It has a more traditional remote shape instead of the curved wand of previous versions. As with all LG TVs, the MRGB95B runs on webOS. It runs quickly, but I find it less intuitive to navigate than Google TV, Apple TV, or Roku. It supports all of the major streaming apps. Some color inaccuracy out of the boxVery distracting reflective screenMore blooming than competitors Speaking of software, a quick note: LG has recently come under increased scrutiny after a video from Gamers Nexus showed security vulnerabilities and continuous audio recording on its TVs, which LG has denied. When setting up the MRGB95B, it’s only necessary to accept the terms of use and privacy policy if you want to use webOS. Otherwise, as with any TV, it can be disconnected from the internet, although an external streaming device would be needed for any streaming services (I use an Apple TV 4K, myself). OK, moving on. So, why buy an RGB LED TV? RGB LED TVs use zones of individual red, green, and blue LEDs as backlights as opposed to a field of white or blue LEDs that rely on a color filter or quantum dots to create the colors that reach our eyes. RGB LED TVs have a color filter, too, but since the backlight is already creating color based on what’s needed onscreen, the color filter doesn’t need to work as hard to dial that in. It allows for higher color brightness and the ability to display more colors. LG (and Samsung) use the term Micro RGB to describe the technology, while others say Mini RGB LED. And while the LG and Samsung RGBs are reportedly smaller (although neither has confirmed the exact size), it doesn’t directly translate into more zones or better performance over Mini RGB. As with all of the RGB LED TVs I’ve seen this year, the 95B gets very bright — I measured a peak of 4,011 nits in HDR Filmmaker mode — allowing it to deliver an impactful image even in a sunlit living room. And in a dark room, the highlights in scenes from The Meg really pop. At the other end of the spectrum, though, the LG struggles a bit. Near-black shadow detail, such as the corners of the living room and kitchen of Sapper Morton’s home at the beginning of Blade Runner 2049, is difficult to see. And when there are bright moments within a dark portion of the screen — subtitles are a good example — the LG exhibits more blooming than I’ve seen on other RGB LED TVs. HDMI inputs: 4 x HDMI 2.1 (one with eARC) Audio support: Dolby Atmos, Dolby Atmos FlexConnect, Dolby Digital, Auracast Gaming features: VRR, ALLM, Game Optimizer, G-Sync compatible The LG 95B can display really vibrant colors thanks to the RGB LED technology. It’s able to cover 100 percent of the P3 color gamut (which is what’s used for films) and nearly 90 percent of the larger BT.2020 color area. But even in Filmmaker mode out of the box, those colors still show visible accuracy issues. Overall, the TV leans toward light blue, so white clouds have a slight blue tint to them and skin tones are cooler than they should be. After a few days living with the LG MRGB95B, I got used to the slightly blue image (although it was still noticeable). A $3,600 TV in 2026 shouldn’t have the inaccuracies on the MRGB95B. While the TV performs well in a bright room during the day, when the sun goes down and the lamps turn on, they can wreak havoc on the image. The 95B’s glossy coating is one of the most reflective I’ve seen in a long time. In moderately lit rooms, a lamp or ceiling light directly reflected on the screen causes rainbows to dance across the image. It’s not as bad when the scene is bright, such as the scorching desert of Arrakis in Dune, but once we enter the dark corridors of House Harkonnen, the reflections become incredibly noticeable. Viewing angles are always a problem for LED TVs — RGB or not — and that’s evident on the LG, too. When only moderately off-axis, the colors become washed out, losing their vibrancy for anyone who’s sitting off to the side. In a vacuum, the MRGB95B is a good TV for certain situations. It’s really bright and stands up to ambient light well (just avoid having ceiling, wall, or standing lights reflected on the screen), and the colors are vibrant if not totally accurate out of the box. But it doesn’t hold up against other TVs. The Sony Bravia 7 II (which you can find $1,300 cheaper at 75 inches) has better color accuracy and handles reflections better. And the Hisense UR9 performs comparably for less money, too. But the real competition comes from within LG itself. The LG G6 OLED is only a few hundred dollars more than the MRGB95B, as is the Samsung S95H, and both of those TVs offer significantly better performance with superior color accuracy and viewing angles. The OLEDs don’t get quite as bright, but since they can turn off light at the pixel level, contrast is remarkable and there’s zero blooming. LG has always struggled to make competitive LED TVs, and that trend continues with the MRGB95B. In fact, it might be LG’s best argument to get an LG OLED TV. I set up each TV in my living room on my home theater credenza. I stream movies and shows through the TV’s apps and from an Apple TV 4K, play discs on my Oppo UDP-203 4K Blu-ray player (including the Spears & Munsil Ultra HD Benchmark disc), and play games on my Xbox Series X and PlayStation 5. This is done at different times of the day and under different lighting conditions, with curtains open, with lamps and overhead lights on, or with blackout curtains up to keep the room dark. While I am a certified ISF Level 3 calibrator, I do not calibrate the TVs before measurement, as the overwhelming majority of TV owners don’t bother. So it’s important to know how well the TVs perform out of the box, with minor tweaks in the menu anyone can do. For measurement, I use Portrait Displays’ Calman color calibration software, a Murideo 8K Seven pattern generator, an X-rite i1 Pro 3 spectrophotometer, Portrait Displays’ C6 HDR5000 colorimeter, a Konica Minolta LS-100 luminance meter, and Leo Bodnar 4K lag tester. The Verge
AI agent makers are promising privacy — will they deliver?
2026-10-10

AI agent makers are promising privacy — will they deliver?

The Verge | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Meta positioned its Muse agent as a safer alternative to OpenClaw. OpenAI promises Dots is a safer alternative to Muse. Meta positioned its Muse agent as a safer alternative to OpenClaw. OpenAI promises Dots is a safer alternative to Muse. At this year’s OpenAI DevDay, CEO Sam Altman unveiled the company’s new AI agent Dots — and told the crowd that the company wants to “set a new standard for privacy in frontier AI.” OpenAI would spend the day taking veiled shots at Meta’s Muse, its primary competitor, for failing to keep users’ data safe. Yet Muse itself, a couple of months earlier, had launched as a supposedly safer alternative to predecessor OpenClaw — with CEO Mark Zuckerberg promising it was “built from the ground up for privacy and security.” In an age when companies hoard customers’ personal data and cyberattacks are a dime a dozen, AI labs are trying to convince users to share even more information with their agents. Their latest strategy for success is one-upping the competition by promising that unlike their rivals, they’ll keep user data away from prying eyes. The question is whether companies can keep these promises. Nat Friedman, head of product at Meta Superintelligence Labs, wrote on X that the company’s “goal with muse was to build something like openclaw that we could make safe and secure and easy to use and scale to billions of people.” User data is stored on a secure VM, what Zuckerberg described as an “isolated linux computer with a browser, CPU, memory, and storage.” The company said that most of the effort in building Muse was related to “careful design and engineering to operate [it] more safely.” Meta’s blog post continued, “Muse can and will still make mistakes, but we expect they’ll be much less frequent and cause much less damage due to the safety systems we’ve built in.” But though Muse topped the App Store charts and, per Apptopia, gained 600,000 daily active users in the US within weeks, Meta’s promises seem to have fallen short. Although data is isolated from other users, and the company plans to introduce a way “to cryptographically and verifiably prevent Meta from accessing data in your VM” later this year, Meta itself can still access the data. A security researcher quickly exposed a zero-day vulnerability that could allow someone to take control of Muse (which has since been patched), raising fears of outside attacks. Multiple serious security issues reportedly cropped up at the last minute before launch, one of which could have allowed users to access Meta’s own internal databases, per 404 Media. Muse also appears to liberally collect (and in some cases, hand out) data. It defaults to allowing Meta to train models on what users put into it, though it’s possible to opt out. An Inc. reporter complained that Muse uploaded and read his private messages without him asking it to, and a YouTuber said it had offered his address to a stranger via Marketplace — in both cases, Muse was functioning apparently as intended, but the user didn’t realize how far it would go. The platform creates “detailed profiles of all your friends and family,” as Wired reported. None of this is necessarily unexpected from Meta, but it certainly doesn’t back up the idea that Muse is uniquely privacy- or security-conscious. OpenAI seized on this fact when announcing Dots in late September. Alexander Embiricos, OpenAI’s Codex product lead, said onstage at DevDay that OpenAI is focused on having the “most trustworthy, safe, and secure assistant,” and CEO Sam Altman demonstrated ways that people could exert control over their individual Dots, such as setting a rule that it should never make a purchase over a certain dollar amount. “I think we’re in a different position to Meta in that they don’t have an AI product that has 1.2 billion users,” said Glen Coates, OpenAI’s head of app platform, adding that “launching something that makes those kinds of mistakes is something that we would try to take the care to avoid.” Altman himself also pushed the privacy angle at DevDay. To court business customers, executives presented a framework allowing enterprises to have “stronger controls” over their data and zero data retention policy options (meaning no data is stored on OpenAI servers). Indeed, so far, there haven’t been many privacy scandals with Dots — although since it’s only available on the $100-and-up ChatGPT subscription tiers, there are also likely fewer people using it. People may still not feel comfortable giving OpenAI their personal data, in part simply because AI agents require so much of it. For instance, The Verge’s Allison Johnson felt uncomfortable typing in her bank information when the bot asked as part of a relevant task (Muse has a Stripe integration to take care of that). Not all companies are making privacy promises, of course. Instinct was publicly criticized for reportedly overly-broad terms of service that gave it unfettered access to data; since then, the company seems to have made adjustments. For now, AI labs seem to be counting on a three-part approach to popularizing AI agent use with the general public: Make them useful, make them cute and disarming to help offset the creepiness, and make promises about privacy — and hope they hold up. The Verge
The Telo MT1 is a big truck trapped in a tiny truck’s body
2026-10-10

The Telo MT1 is a big truck trapped in a tiny truck’s body

The Verge | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Tiny pickup trucks might just become a thing in the US if the California-based startup Telo has its way. Tiny pickup trucks might just become a thing in the US if the California-based startup Telo has its way. I’m bouncing down the road in San Carlos, California, zipping past behemoth tech companies like EA and Oracle, in America’s smallest electric truck. The Telo MT1 (which stands for “mini truck”) unquestionably turns heads because it looks so remarkably different from everything else on the market right now. It has a nearly cab-over design, meaning that front-seat passengers sit almost directly over the front axle. It looks a lot like a Japanese kei truck, with a super-short nose and four doors. People stop to stare as we sit at stoplights or roll silently past. The interior is surprisingly spacious, and while the seats are firm, they’re not uncomfortable, at least for short jaunts on the bumpy roads around Telo’s HQ. Telo Trucks cofounder and CEO Jason Marks drove us to the company’s offices ahead of my short one-block test drive, and from the passenger seat, the ride was firm but stable, thanks to a low center of gravity provided by the battery pack beneath the floor. Behind the wheel, the MT1 feels like a lighter, more nimble pickup truck with a stiff, slightly bouncy ride without any payload in the bed, combined with all the low-end power, torque, and acceleration of an EV. Once we circle back around to the road in front of Telo’s offices, Marks has me come to a full stop and stomp on the accelerator to get a feel for just how much power the little truck puts down, remarking that this was the same road where Tesla used to do its customer test drives of the Roadster many years ago. People stop to stare as we sit at stoplights Marks says that the production version of the Telo will accelerate 0–60mph in an estimated four seconds. The truck easily rockets from a standstill, just like any other electric vehicle on the market today, a feat for a small startup with just 30 employees. Despite its diminutive appearance, the tiny Telo truck can go toe-to-toe with the giant SUVs and Teslas that populate this area. The Telo is, as Marks likes to point out, actually the same size as a two-door Mini Cooper, but far more practical with a tow rating of 8,000lbs, a full-size bed (96 inches), and a bevy of neat hidden features that Telo hopes will offer a viable, if somewhat pricey, alternative to the giant pickup trucks that dominate the American market. “I hope that our truck shows how amazing and fun and useful — especially useful — a small pickup truck can be, because the current big trucks are literally killing us,” Forrest North, Telo’s cofounder and CTO, said during our interview at Telo’s headquarters. “I mean, they’re fun to be up super high and look out and feel like you’re dominating the world, but they’re killing an extra, roughly, 5,000 people per year, so do we want to just give them a pass for that?” Marks and North both live and work in the San Francisco Bay Area with their families, and both own pickup trucks: Marks because he has a 150lb three-legged dog he shuttles back and forth to the office every day, and North because he has a passion for the outdoors. Large pickup trucks can be useful, but they’re far from suitable for city life. That disconnect, as well as the founders’ passion for urban transportation, is what brought the two, along with Yves Behar, together to start Telo in 2022. Marks previously worked on automotive safety and validation at National Instruments, while North is a former battery engineer at Tesla, founder of Mission Motors (one of the first electric motorcycle companies to break the land speed record for an electric motorcycle), and cofounder of Recargo, which developed EV charging app PlugShare. Behar is the founder of fuseproject, an industrial design and brand development firm. Marks and North met through a mutual friend in the startup space and discovered they’d gone to the same high school in Washington, where they both pole-vaulted. It was a “kindred spirit moment,” according to Marks. “I hope that our truck shows how amazing and fun and useful — especially useful — a small pickup truck can be, because the current big trucks are literally killing us.” Originally, their idea wasn’t about trucks at all — it was about electric motorcycles. Their initial pitch to an unnamed Silicon Valley VC firm included a slide deck comparing three concepts: an electric motorcycle, a small car, and a tiny truck. Everyone they talked to kept gravitating toward the tiny pickup. Sensing the enthusiasm, North pushed Marks to consider pivoting to building a truck, even though the motorcycle they’d planned was nearly complete. They even went so far as to poll about 200 people on the street and found that nearly 90 percent would rather have the truck. The next week, they pivoted from motorcycle to truck. “It’s clear to me that the trucks have just kind of jumped the shark,” North said. “The only thing they can do now is be big.” Telo aims to change the conversation about what electric transportation in urban centers can look like. For a small truck, the Telo MT1 packs a lot of range. At launch, the truck will come with a 77kWh battery pack, or a larger 106kWh pack that the company estimates will deliver up to 350 miles of range. The MT1 has a unique battery layout: a split pack with both 800V and 400V architecture. The 400V setup makes the Telo chargeable on Tesla’s V3 Supercharger infrastructure, while the 800V will let customers take advantage of new, higher-powered stations coming to market in areas like San Francisco. Telo currently builds its battery packs in-house. Each cell is individually fused so that a single failure won’t trigger a cascade across the pack. According to Marks, in-house manufacturing allows them to maximize energy density and longevity — a feat that hinges on thermal management. The passive thermal management system operates while the vehicle is parked, maintaining cooler temperatures even in direct sunlight, while active liquid cooling engages only when the vehicle is running. Additionally, North noted that Telo’s latest cells deliver roughly 20 percent more energy than those they sourced just a year ago. For a small truck, the Telo MT1 packs a lot of range I drove one of two prototypes that Telo has been using for road testing. From the outside, the Telo looks smaller than its 152 inches of length suggest. With the lower rear section of the passenger seats (called the midgate) laid flat, you get a full-size, 96-inch truck bed. To make up for the lack of a frunk, which most EVs have, Telo also developed a “monster” tunnel (named because, like in a kids’ story, it’s a “monster under the bed”), which passes door to door beneath the front of the truck bed. It’s accessible from either side and is large enough for a pair of roll-aboard carry-on suitcases to slide in endwise. Marks said that while they knew the front of the MT1 would be difficult to engineer, adding the monster tunnel created a tough engineering challenge because it sits exactly where the battery pack connects. The interior is still being finalized, but Marks said that it will get two 13-inch digital displays that will support both Android Auto and Apple CarPlay. The steering wheel also has small scroll wheels, but most features will be controlled through the screen interface. Japanese kei vehicles became a White House talking point late last year when President Donald Trump invited the tiny trucks to be built in the US. But making that actually happen is far more complicated because kei vehicles don’t meet US crash safety regulations. Marks claimed that Telo has solved this problem with a completely new front end and an innovative front hinge pillar that took nearly four years to develop and hundreds of thousands of hours of simulated crashes to verify. In the Telo, the redesigned hinge pillar does the structural job of a conventional one while also carrying the strut mount, the cross-car beam, and the dash — consolidating several separate parts into one. This allowed the company to cut the front-end part count roughly in half, Marks said. “We pretty much threw all [the conventional crash architecture] away and had to start from scratch,” Marks said. The company’s team includes Tesla’s former head of crash safety, brought on to help tackle the new front end and completely reengineer it. “We have effectively six crash [paths], two bumper beams and two shock structures, a completely different architecture of a hinge pillar, a completely different architecture of a torque box to distribute the energy appropriately to the various parts of the car and save that occupant compartment as much as possible,” he said, comparing the crash innovations to those in a Smartcar. “Would any other automaker say we’re going to bet the world on being able to rethink crash?” The company’s team includes Tesla’s former head of crash safety AI tools have made the virtual crash-testing process much more affordable for startups, and Telo recently locked in its body in white, the sheet metal shell made up of the major structural pieces of the truck (think of it like a skeleton of a vehicle). It’s currently being built by US supplier Schwab Industries in Michigan. Marks said Telo uses AI to run virtual crash tests around the clock, averaging roughly one test per hour. The next phase of crash testing will include cutting the body in white apart for what Marks called a “quasi-static validation,” and full crash testing (slamming the vehicle and its various parts into a wall to validate the virtual testing the company has done) will start next year before Telo starts customer deliveries. “I think it’s one of the most exciting times to start a hardware company,” North said. “It’s always been like, hardware is hard. That’s always been the cliché, and I wouldn’t say hardware is easy, but I would say it’s gotten so much easier because of software and AI.” The Telo MT1 will come in single-motor (rear-wheel drive) and dual-motor (all-wheel drive) layouts and offer add-ons like a solar-capable tonneau cover or camping shell. Reservations are open now for a refundable $152, and the base vehicle starts at $41,520. Marks said the company isn’t really worried about larger competitors like Ford’s upcoming midsized electric truck or Slate’s bare-bones EV because he sees their market entry as proof that a viable market exists for small electric pickup trucks. North argued that Telo is also in a better position than either Slate or Ford right now because the company has fewer parts moving in and out of the country; it’s assembling its own battery packs and building the body in white stateside, making it largely exempt from the Trump administration’s tariffs. “The pack is the most expensive part of the vehicle build and the heart and soul of the vehicle,” Marks said, “so we had to keep it in-house.” Marks said he’s been surprised by the volume of reservations: just over 14,000, with 70 percent of reservation-holders configuring AWD versions with the longer-range battery pack. He also noted that around 20 percent are first-time car buyers. Telo is aiming for profitability at around 5,000 vehicles within roughly 24 months of deliveries. The company plans to get vehicles into customers’ hands sometime in 2027, depending on the validation timelines, but the MT1 still has to go through EPA and California air quality certification. Most reservations have come from places like LA, New York, the Bay Area, Seattle, Dallas, Washington, DC, Miami, and Denver, with almost everyone opting for the larger battery pack, regardless of drive configuration. Marks pointed to Tesla as the trailblazer of EVs’ first real wave, proving electric cars could be fast, fun, and sporty. However, he believes the industry ultimately missed the chance to fully exploit their potential. Telo is tackling the problem by rethinking vehicle architecture from the ground up, returning to a “one-box” design made possible by eliminating wasted space. Ultimately, Telo’s focus comes down to purpose, according to Marks: “What does electric enable for the experience of the user, and what value does that bring?” While history has shown that the market isn’t kind to EV startups, in a world where giant American trucks increasingly barely fit in an average parking spot, the Telo MT1 and its creators are aiming to offer a capable electric pickup built for today’s cramped cities in the hope that Americans can get behind smaller and more capable electric vehicles. The director of Fjord takes the ‘risky position’ of moderator
2026-10-10

The director of Fjord takes the ‘risky position’ of moderator

The Verge | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
With his new film about a Romanian family’s life in Norway, Cristian Mungiu wants you to consider the limits of your tolerance﻿ With his new film about a Romanian family’s life in Norway, Cristian Mungiu wants you to consider the limits of your tolerance﻿ Cristian Mungiu’s sixth feature, Fjord, concerns a family on trial. They seem like good people, God-fearing. They’ve just moved from Romania to a small hamlet nestled in the fjords of rural Norway. There is Mamma (Renate Reinsve), Tata (a bald and bespectacled Sebastian Stan), and their passel of children. They are an openly religious and proselytizing family. The parents, unfashionably, believe in punitive consequences to actions. They would never be accused of gentle parenting. All ought to be well, only Norway in 2026 is extravagantly suspicious of the devout — their famous tolerance reserved for the secular. Fjord starts quietly and builds subtly. We are with the family as they are introduced to their places of learning, employment, and worship. A second, Norwegian, family serves as their foil. Sort of. Questions arise, about what counts as discipline, what as punishment. To those wanting answers, objective analysis, which is to say a kind of measured purity, Mungiu says, come down to the mud with us! In the morally complex world of the movie, nothing is spared — a piano chord, a passing remark, hems of clothes — all are cast in a different light. We are all the more enriched for it. The Verge spoke with Mungiu, who is himself Romanian, about how his early tenure in journalism informs his writing, what it was like living under Nicolae Ceaușescu’s dictatorial reign, Marxist literary theory, and the challenges and pleasures of thinking for oneself. This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity. I’d like to start with you at Cannes, where you said the question that Fjord raises is: Are there good minorities and bad minorities? I wonder if this question has changed for you since. Not so much. I think that as much as we need to understand that there are good values in society, like tolerance for all the other minorities and inclusion of those who are different than us, we should always focus on this idea that sometimes even if there are minorities with whom we don’t share the same values, we need to be tolerant to those as well. If not, what makes us believe that we have a more liberal, progressive view on the world? Of course, there is a limit. We all need to condemn violence, and we all need to be on the same side of helping people out. But whenever we talk about, for example, religious beliefs, even if we are people who don’t share such beliefs, it’s good to be tolerant with them, if they want to share different views. You need to let them be. “Even if there are minorities with whom we don’t share the same values, we need to be tolerant to those as well.” There are so many people next to us who share a lot of irrational beliefs, from reading the horoscope to believing in alternative medicine. We let them be. Why would we condemn people for being religious? It’s good to try to convince them that actually it’s good to see that your beliefs are based on something rational, because eventually, we all influence the lives of others, and it’s good to make choices based on rational things. But you need to advance little by little, step by step. I don’t believe in this way of imposing views on others and making rules for others to change what they think. If you do that, you will only radicalize people. You said you kept chancing upon articles about immigrant families in Nordic countries. Is reading the news part of your practice? Otherwise, where have your ideas come from? I read a lot of news every day, and I keep a lot of these articles in special folders under different themes. And it took years before I decided to make a film based on the ideas in these articles, because in the beginning they are very spectacular. They are just informing us about something spectacular that has happened. While for me, the film should be something deeper, more profound, in the sense that I’m trying to figure out why those things have happened and what do they tell us about the world around us? I try to avoid the sensational part of it and to try to dig down, and to understand what is it that it could tell you about your own life, and this is what I did for the original story as well. I needed some eight years from the moment when I read about it to the moment when I decided to make a film out of it. Tell me more about your excavation process. Can you walk us through what is actually happening in those eight years? And specifically, can you tell us about the moment in the process when you go from research to fiction? What does that leap look like, or maybe it’s seamless? It’s fair to say that I haven’t used all these 10 years to investigate. But I was aware all the time that this exists. For example, I decided to talk to this family who was involved in the most prominent case, in the most spoken-about case, from three years ago. I talked to them. I talked to their children. I went to Norway. I met their relatives. I went to see the house where everything happened. I took advantage in that moment to investigate more, and I met everybody that I could who was involved in that case. And people who were willing to talk to me. I talked to lawyers. I talked to all the press that covered the case. I talked to the translator they were having when talking to the police. I talked to the Barnevernet [Norwegian child welfare agency]. I talked to the NGOs who were involved a lot in such cases. Later on, I was a witness to the trials they were having on children issues. So I did my best to find all the points of view of people involved, not only in that case but in other cases. And finally, I crafted the screenplay based a lot on the arguments that I was recording. I didn’t have to invent much. I just needed to organize the material, but I didn’t need to build a lot on what are the arguments of each of these two sides. The fictional part of the film comes a lot from this second family, which is fully fictional, and that gave me the possibility of following the film from the point of view of the adults. I needed to be with the family, but I wanted to balance this point of view of the family with the point of view of another family with children and sharing the values of the society in which they live, a family with whom they need to communicate with because they are secluded in this fjord at the end of the world, and it was important for me to see how these values collide, and what changes in this process of being forced to communicate with others. Tell us about your time as a reporter, early in your career. How did you decide to turn to film? I started working in the press in 1987, and also this was a part of me getting closer to what I wanted to do. I wished that I could become a storyteller, and I wished already at that age — I was 19 — that I could become a filmmaker. Back then, becoming a student at the film school during communist times wasn’t really a fair process, so it wasn’t for me. So I was starting to get closer to becoming a writer. This is what I thought I could do. I started writing. And then I got to this newspaper, and I started practicing my writing and taking pictures. Two activities which were as close as I could to becoming a filmmaker without being able to become one on the spot. A lot of my style of writing screenplays today is still based on that period in which I learned how to write a piece of news. But happily, you know, the wall fell. Communism collapsed. It was very passionate to be in the press for a while because people were hungry for what they were calling “truth” after such a long period in which we didn’t have a free press. My style of writing was shaped in that period. I am not bringing, when I describe things, my own point of view. I’m describing an external situation, the way in which these things manifest, but I don’t draw the conclusion. I depict a situation, and I encourage you to use your critical spirit to understand the way it manifests. I am encouraging you also to take on this responsibility as a spectator, in which I’m not guiding you through the story. You have your freedom of interpreting based on facts, and making sure you analyze the facts. If you’re just analyzing what the characters say, you might be very easily manipulated. Manipulated, because, as you know, in cinema, characters lie, and are as manipulative as people are in real lives. So it’s not that you wanted to be a filmmaker as such, but that you wanted to be a storyteller, and you found film? I wanted to be a storyteller, and I thought that I’m going to be a writer. Then I was undergoing this period in my life in which I was watching a lot of films, very popular films. Also being influenced by the Romanian films that I could watch in the period, which were very strange. Maybe it was, in part, the censorship, but probably it was also that the people that were encouraged to make films were not very talented. But they seemed to be made with people looking like us, and apparently using the same language as us, but actually nothing like that ever happened in reality. They were psychologically so unbelievable, and the dialogue was also a message that somebody wrote in the right order grammatically, and it was pure propaganda. So, we started developing this idea that it shouldn’t be this complicated to make films. You just need to co-opt reality. I want to tell you something funny about the message that I got today. Following a lot of these cases in the Nordic countries and talking to so many people, I became some sort of an expert. I got a message this morning from a half-German, half-Norwegian family saying, Help. Help us. Nothing has changed. I’m trying to bring this forward. This is a topic about which we all need to have an opinion. But I’m not this superhero that can solve the situation. It’s important to make this clear that the film is not just about Romania and Norway and the Barnevernet. The film is about what happens in the States too, for example. I think that this polarization of people needs to have an answer because we can’t continue living in these bubbles. This degree of hatred and aggression, which might end up badly, and very violent. What do you do when people call you for help? I started this promotional tour in Norway, and I met a lot of people from both sides talking to me. We ran across this very difficult confession of a spectator, this lady who was crying, telling us that she was experiencing these cases with such emotion that finally she decided, for fear of having her children taken away by the authorities, she decided to rather have an abortion. We also met people telling us that they are 100 percent behind the point of view of the protection in the film, because if they were in the situation of having their children taken in a conservative society, nobody would care for them. I think this shows that people rather tend to imagine that the wrong and radical way of seeing the society can be compensated by another wrong and radical way, and this shouldn’t be the answer. The answer should be more attempts at integration, trying to be more empathic and more open to dialogue, if we wish to give a good example to the part of the society who think they can’t escape this idea that they already know the truth and there’s no way out. In the court scene, Sebastian Stan’s character gestures at the ways in which liberal societies privilege physical harm over emotional or verbal harm. There is, of course, no such thing as a view from nowhere. I know you said your opinion doesn’t matter, but I would still like to know how you relate or do not relate to this argument. Of course, we need to make this clear. As soon as there is proven violence in a family, we all need to act as soon as we can and save them. Be it children or women. Of course, we need to act. But the film talks about the fact that in most cases, you are supposed to make a choice, not knowing what happened in the family. You are just having assumptions in most of these cases, and this is why Fjord is crafted as such. After investigating for two years and talking to all the parties involved, I can’t tell if this real family, which was involved in the real case, was violent or not. I don’t know. I’m not saying that they were not. I don’t know, because even if I talk to them and to their children... But I noticed that when people are making choices in Norway, for example, or us in society in general, we tend to be biased by our own stereotypes about the situation, and it’s very difficult to position yourself in an objective point of view. This doesn’t really exist. “I was curious enough to see what happens in liberal, progressive societies when the conservatives all of a sudden become a minority. ” So you need to be aware that you’re biased, that you are seeing the situation from your perspective, and when you’re mentioning that you’re doing whatever is best for the benefit of the children, actually you tend to believe that your solution is for the best, while the others will believe the same if they act on their opinions. And of course, you know, we need to make sure that there is enough freedom accepted by everybody into our societies, so that minorities have the right to be who they want to be. But after making a film that was speaking a lot about how minorities are being discriminated in conservative society, I was curious enough to see what happens in liberal, progressive societies when the conservatives all of a sudden become a minority. Wouldn’t you expect that they are treated with more empathy for not sharing the same values with the majority? Well, actually, they are not. The fact that you belong to the majority tends to be more important to you than the values that belong to your ideology. Literary theorist Terry Eagleton has a quote that goes, “Ideology is like bad breath. We only recognize it in other people.” I understand precise choreography is part of your process. You have talked about how you also try your best to avoid cutting because that is an interpretation. Does that mean the point of art is verisimilitude, capturing reality? And if not that, what are movies for? Why do you make movies? I believe in diversity in filmmaking, and I think that making entertainment is also a valid way of making films, but it shouldn’t be the only way. The films that I try to make are films that try to make people think a little bit more, and to make them aware of the world which exists next to theirs. I’m using this as a tool to make you aware that you should be very careful of what happens next to you. There isn’t anything like an objective point of view, but the best I could do is not to be manipulative with my style. I’m not making a film biased by one of these points of view. I’m taking this risky position of a moderator, bringing two sets of values in front of you so that you can double check your values and to remember when was the last time when you doubted that you are on the good side of things? Everybody else is so convinced that they are as right as you, but you know the first step into entering some sort of a dialogue is listening to others. Fjord was released in select US theaters October 9th, with a wide release on October 23rd. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. My brief romance with an AI bird feeder

My brief romance with an AI bird feeder

The Verge | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. It started off promising enough. Dozens of tiny, colorful birds were drawn to my garden just a few weeks after I mounted a $350 $269 Kiwibit Bird Feeder 2 Pro. “Looks like Great Tit!” read the first AI-driven alert sent to my phone. “Looks like Eurasian Blue Tit!” read another. I chuckled, still waiting for adulthood to kick in. This went on for days. Hundreds of miniature tits came to snack on expensive sunflower seeds already husked. They’d skulk about in nearby trees before flittering in to grab a single seed to be safely enjoyed in the canopy above. I took great delight in my new house guests. I admired them in crisp 4K HDR video recordings. I looked for unique markings in the hope of naming my new feral pets. I clicked deeper into the Kiwibit app to read about my visitors’ migration habits, where they nest, and what they like to eat. It was all going swell until he arrived. “Looks like Common Wood Pigeon!” came the alert. All I saw was a battleship-gray blob obscuring the live camera feed. The winged rat was so fat and imposing that he blocked the lens. Ugh, no, go back to the tourists! In mild panic, I triggered the feeder’s Nuisance Animal Alarm, a feature typically reserved for squirrels and the like. The pigeon tensed, looked around, but wasn’t deterred by the audible siren that was surely annoying my neighbors. Soon it went back to gorging on the haute cuisine purchased for more refined guests. It ate and ate and ate. For 15 minutes, it ate until I walked outside and shooed it away. Thereafter, that pigeon, or pigeons (who can tell) moved in. They’d perch for 30 minutes or more, slamming their faces into the seed pile with the clumsy enthusiasm of a tween bobbing for apples. Mice arrived below to feast on the spoils. Admitting defeat, I dismounted the expensive bird feeder and shoved it into a closet. I’m not mad at Kiwibit. The “Bird Feeder 2 Pro 4K AI Camera with Solar Panel (Lifetime AI Included)” I tested was a joy to use, until it wasn’t. That’s not Kiwibit’s fault. If you’ve been tempted to buy a smart bird feeder, I’d suggest starting with something simple, inexpensive, and without AI instead of going all in on day one — you can’t return a used feeder just because it attracts pests.
